# Commit notes — the per-fix record (moved out of CLAUDE.md §11 on 2026-09-15)

Every `C###` note lives here, **newest first**, verbatim as it was written into `CLAUDE.md` §11 at the time. CLAUDE.md §11 keeps the standing items (backlog, dead schema, security ladder, product ideas) and points here. **New notes are inserted at the top of this file**, before the previous newest — the same rule §11 had. `docs/notes/FINDINGS.md` holds the closed drives and `O###` diagnoses; `docs/notes/MIGRATIONS.md` holds the per-migration verification narratives.

- **★★★ C312 — THE COCKPIT NAV IS A SIDEBAR, AND THE TEN ROWS WERE NEVER THE PROBLEM (2026-09-10). EIGHT MUTATIONS, EIGHT REDS.**
  - **★★★ THE DEFECT WAS SHAPE, NOT COUNT: TEN DESTINATIONS YOU COULD ONLY SEE AFTER CLICKING A TAB THAT DID NOT NAME THEM, IN A STRIP THAT SCROLLED SIDEWAYS.** §11 records the cost twice on one drive — the operator, who wrote the fixture, repeatedly could not find the Matching Engine and stalled on Bank Import. **A horizontal strip has no room for grouping, so hierarchy has to be spent on HIDING things.** A column has room, so every destination this seat may open is on screen at once, under a heading that says what it is for.
  - **★★★ AND THE TWO SCREENS THAT HAD NO ROW AT ALL ARE THE PROOF THE OLD SHAPE WAS OUT OF SPACE. `MatchingView` was reachable only from a post-booking redirect and one CONDITIONAL link on Home — so on a day it had nothing to say, there was no door to it.** `ArView` is a real 315-line receivables aging screen with AI narration and it was reachable from **nowhere**, while Payables had a row the whole time. **Nothing ever displayed the pair, which is how an asymmetry that obvious survived.**
  - **★★ THE GROUPS ARE THE DOMAIN'S OWN DIVISIONS — Ledger · Money in · Money out · Bank · Records.** Putting money in beside money out is what makes a missing Receivables row visible; a tidier alphabetical list would have hidden it exactly as well as the old one did.
  - **★ NOTHING WAS REMOVED. A test asserts every destination the flat ten offered is still one click away**, because *"we grouped it"* and *"we dropped it"* look identical from a screenshot. **O86(h) — Reconcile collapsing to exceptions-only — stays UNSHIPPED and correctly so:** §11 sequences it strictly after confidence calibration, and calibration has not switched over. **This moves walls; it does not front-run the pipeline.**
  - **★★ A NEW INVARIANT, AND IT IS THE ONE THIS FEATURE IS FOR: EVERY SIDEBAR ROW MUST BE A VIEW THAT SEAT MAY ACTUALLY OPEN.** Pinned for both seats over every row. **A row the guard then refuses is worse than no row — it teaches you that clicking is how you find out**, which is `O124`'s rule applied to navigation.
  - **★★ SEVEN EXISTING `iaCollapse` TESTS WENT RED, ALL CORRECTLY, AND REPOINTING THEM WAS THE RISKY PART.** They pinned `navSeat.tabs` and `booksSubtabs`, and the shape moved. **Each was re-aimed at the PROPERTY it protects** — what a seat can reach, that preview only ever subtracts, that the chrome reads the one helper — never at the shape that happened to carry it. **A test quietly dropped along with the mechanism it named is how a guarantee is lost while the suite stays green.**
  - **★ AND MY FIRST NEW GUARD FAILED FOR A REASON IT DID NOT MEAN — THE SIXTH TIME IN THIS REPO.** It banned the row labels anywhere in `App.jsx` and tripped on a misroute confirmation, *"routed it to Bank Import"* — a sentence about a destination, not a nav row. Scoped to the `<nav>` block, **and made to refuse a slice that found nothing**, so it cannot pass by matching an empty string.
  - **★★ ONE BEHAVIOUR CHANGE, STATED AS ONE: THE TAB AND SUB-NAV HANDLERS DISAGREED ABOUT FILTER RESETS.** The tab reset the vendor filter on every click and forced `booksFilter` to "all"; the sub-nav cleared `booksFilter` only when it happened to be "contracts". **So one destination behaved two ways depending on which row you took to get there, and a row labelled Transactions could land you on a filtered list.** One rule now — the tab's, because it is the predictable one.
  - **▶ FOUND AND NOT ACTED ON, DELIBERATELY: `ledger`, `money-in` and `money-out` ARE DEAD ROUTER BRANCHES.** All three are `setView("books")` side effects **called during render**, with zero `setView` call sites anywhere and the AI navigate map already aliasing them to `books`. **`O96`'s class exactly** — dead plumbing that looks wired because it sits in `BOOKS_GROUP`. Left alone: deleting views is a different change from moving walls, and mixing them makes the diff impossible to judge. **`invoices` (`InvoicesView`) is a fourth, milder case** — reachable from a detail back-link and Onboard, but with no nav row, so its back button lands you somewhere the sidebar cannot highlight.
  - **▶ AND THE CLIENT'S THIRD SURFACE IS STILL MISSING, WHICH THE ROADMAP DID NOT SAY.** Phase 2's specced client nav is *"Home, Reports, and a read-only Activity/Documents view"*; ROADMAP recorded the whole item as ✅ SHIPPED at C197 while `docs` has always been reviewer-only. **A client drops a file and can never see it again.** Activity IS on Home (`ownerActivityText`), so what is actually owed is Documents. Now carried as its own open row rather than inside an item marked done.

- **★★★ C313 — THE CLIENT SEAT WAS CUT TOO FAR, AND THE EVIDENCE NEVER SAID TO CUT THAT FAR (2026-09-10). FOUR MUTATIONS, FOUR REDS.**
  - **★★★ OPERATOR, AND IT IS THE RIGHT READ: *"we don't need a bunch of different windows and shit for different users… this is getting too complicated. We are losing the plot."*** C197 left a business owner with **two screens**, and the O83 evidence behind the collapse was **every one of it about OPERATING a workbench** — the operator stalled on Bank Import's checkbox states and could not find the Matching Engine. **None of it was about LOOKING AT YOUR OWN RECORDS.**
  - **★★★ SO THE LINE IS VERB-SHAPED, NOT SEAT-SHAPED: RECORDS YOU *READ* ARE YOURS; WORKFLOWS YOU *OPERATE* ARE THE CPA'S.** Client seat is now **Home · Transactions · Customers · Vendors · Documents · Reports** — flat, no headings. Bank Import, Reconcile, Matching, Payroll and the Review queue stay in the cockpit, and that is the whole of what the drives actually justified.
  - **★★ THE COST OF THE OVER-CUT, STATED PLAINLY: A CLIENT WHO CAN SEE TWO SCREENS HAS TO ASK THEIR ACCOUNTANT TO LOOK THINGS UP FOR THEM** — which is precisely the dependency this product exists to remove. *"What did I spend", "who do I buy from", "where did my receipt go"* are owner questions, and every one of them was behind a locked door.
  - **★★★ AND THE ONE DOOR OUT OF HOME HAD TO CHANGE WITH IT, OR THE WIDENING WOULD HAVE SHIPPED DEAD LINKS.** `goCockpit` refused **any** client outright — correct while a client had two screens, **wrong the moment they had six**: the anomaly cards fall back to `setView("books")`, so a seat-shaped refusal would leave a client clicking a row **on their own home page** and getting nothing. It is now `navTo` and asks **the seat's own view list**, so the door widens exactly as the seat does with no second list to keep in step — the same list the sidebar reads.
  - **★ RENAMED, BECAUSE THE NAME WAS THE DOCUMENTATION.** A function still called *"go to the cockpit"* that now opens a client's own records is a comment that lies in the one place nobody re-reads.
  - **★★ A COMMENT OF MINE CLAIMED SOMETHING THE CODE DID NOT DO, AND IT IS THE §9 CLASS COMMITTED IN A COMMENT.** I listed *"who owes me"* among the owner questions this answers — **`ar` is not in the client set.** Corrected, and Payables/Receivables are now named as *deliberately not included*: both are read-shaped and both would follow from the same argument, **but a nav row nobody chose is how a boundary drifts.** Left for someone to decide out loud.
  - **▶ ONE CONSEQUENCE WORTH KNOWING: THIS IS A VISIBILITY CHANGE THAT RE-EXPOSES WRITE CONTROLS TO THE OWNER ROLE.** Transactions carries the removal control, which is **role**-gated (`isViewer`/`isMember`), not seat-gated — so an owner can again remove an entry from their own books. That is the pre-C197 state and it is safe by construction (soft delete, audit row, Undo, and `078` refuses a signed month at the database), but it was not true yesterday.

- **★★ C314 — THE CLIENT GETS PAYABLES AND RECEIVABLES, AND THEY DO NOT SAY "PAYABLES" (2026-09-10). FOUR MUTATIONS, FOUR REDS.** Operator's call, and the obvious next one after `C313` — *"who do I owe"* and *"who owes me"* are owner questions by exactly the argument that widened the seat.
  - **★★★ BUT THOSE TWO WORDS ARE THE TWO §11's STANDING DIRECTIVE EXISTS FOR — *every owner-facing surface assumes ZERO accounting knowledge* — and `OWNER_JARGON_RE` NAMES BOTH BY HAND.** So the client's rows read **"Bills to pay"** and **"Money owed to you"**; the cockpit keeps Payables and Receivables, because §9 exempts reviewer-facing copy and **a CPA reading "Bills to pay" is being talked down to.** Same screens, different words — **and it cost no machinery at all, because the client's row list was already a separate array from the cockpit's.**
  - **★★★ AND THE JARGON GUARD HAD A HOLE THE SIZE OF AN "s": `\bpayable\b` DOES NOT MATCH "Payables".** The word boundary fails against the trailing plural — **so the form a UI actually uses walked straight through the check for as long as it has existed**, while the singular nobody writes on a button was caught. Widened to `\bpayables?\b` / `\breceivables?\b`. **Found only because this change put the word on a client surface and I checked the guard instead of assuming it covered me** — the guard would have said yes.
  - **★★ A NEW STANDING CHECK, AND IT IS THE ONE THAT WOULD HAVE CAUGHT THIS BEFORE I DID: EVERY CLIENT-VISIBLE NAV LABEL MUST PASS `containsOwnerJargon`.** The CPA's are asserted to KEEP their accounting words in the same test, so *"make the copy plainer"* can never quietly flatten the cockpit too.

- **★★★ C315 — I MOVED A WALL AND DID NOT READ THE ROOMS BEHIND IT (2026-09-10). SIX MUTATIONS, SIX REDS.** `C313`/`C314` took the client seat from two screens to eight, and **every one of the six it added was written for a CPA.** Moving a wall is one commit; what is on the other side of it is six files nobody re-read.
  - **★★★ THE WORST OF IT WAS LIVE AND NOT COSMETIC: A BUSINESS OWNER OPENING TRANSACTIONS SAW A BANK-RECONCILIATION PANEL.** `{(reconciliations||[]).length>0 && …}` — gated on nothing but *"are there any"* — so **the one workflow the North Star is most explicit about keeping away from a client was sitting on their own records screen** from the moment I pushed. Now `cockpit &&`, with the ASC 842 contract detail and the hand-entry button beside it.
  - **★★ TWO DEAD CONTROLS, WHICH IS `O124` IN NAVIGATION FORM — an action that refuses on click teaches you that clicking is how you find out.** *"+ Add entry"* → `add` and the contract row → `contracts`; both bounce a client Home via the route guard.
  - **★★★ AND THE THIRD DEAD CONTROL PREDATES ALL OF THIS BY MONTHS. `DetailView`'s *"View all invoices for X"* pointed at `invoices` — a view NO SEAT HAS A NAV ROW FOR.** `detail` has been client-visible since `C197`, so **a client has been bounced Home from that button ever since**, and a CPA "succeeded" onto a screen the sidebar cannot highlight. **Found by writing the check, not by reading the file** — which several people have edited since. It goes to `books` + the vendor filter now: same rows, on the screen both seats actually have.
  - **★★★ THE DURABLE PART IS THE CHECK, AND IT ASKS THE TWO QUESTIONS NOBODY WAS ASKING: of every view a client can open — (1) does any control lead somewhere this seat cannot go, and (2) does its copy assume accounting knowledge?** `tests/clientSurfaces.test.js`. **The next person who widens `CLIENT_VIEW_IDS` is told what they have exposed**, which is the thing I did not have this morning.
  - **★★ TWO OF THE "COPY DEFECTS" WERE GUARD DEFECTS, AND FIXING THE GUARD WAS THE RIGHT ANSWER BOTH TIMES.** *(1)* **`1099` IS FOUR DIGITS STARTING 1, SO `OWNER_GLCODE_RE` READ AN IRS FORM AS A CHART-OF-ACCOUNTS NUMBER** — every string in the 1099 feature flagged as though we were showing an owner a GL code. Excluded **by name, not by widening the shape**, since the shape is the whole point; `1040`/`1065`/`1120` are deliberately NOT excluded, because none is used today and **the guard firing is the correct and visible way to find out if one ever is.** *(2)* `\bcredit(ed|s)?\b` matched **"Credit Card"** in a payment-method list. **Seventh comment-or-copy guard in this repo to fail for a reason it does not mean, and the first fixed in the regex itself rather than around it.**
  - **★★ THE EXEMPTIONS ARE BY EXACT STRING, EACH SAYING WHY, AND A TEST FAILS IF ONE GOES STALE.** *"Balance Sheet"* stays — **a financial statement's proper name is not an assumed concept, and renaming it would leave a client unable to ask their accountant for it by name.** *"Trial Balance"*, the two `TOTAL OUTSTANDING …` headings and *"AI Confidence"* are **pre-existing debt on surfaces that were the client's before this change**, pointed at `TIER 1 #7`/`#7a` rather than swallowed. **An unexplained allow-list is the same as no rule** (`C249`'s excused tables, same shape) — and a second check asserts every exemption is still REACHED, so a fixed string cannot leave its licence lying open.
  - **★ AND THE CHECK HAD TO LEARN TO READ A GATED BLOCK, OR IT WOULD HAVE REPORTED THE FIX AS THE DEFECT.** BooksView's CPA copy is inside `{cockpit && ( … )}`, so the strip is **brace-matched, not regex'd** — a lazy match stops at the first brace in a JSX block and silently keeps the rest. Pinned by a planted case asserting the block goes and its neighbours stay. **And a run of style props came back as a "label" reading `, fontSize:13 }}>{a.credit?…` and was flagged for containing "credit"** — filtered by SHAPE (a label never contains `{}<>=;`) rather than allow-listed, because the string was noise and the class was not.

- **★★★ C316 — THE 1099 PLAN COULD NOT FIND MOST VENDORS' PAYMENTS, AND THE BADGE BESIDE IT WAS A BUTTON THAT DID NOTHING (2026-09-10). FIVE MUTATIONS, FIVE REDS.** Found by asking what a client can now *do* on the screens `C313` exposed — the buttons, having already checked the copy and the links.
  - **★★★ THE LOOKUP HAD TWO NORMALISATIONS AND THEY NEVER MET.** `TaxView` keyed its payment map on the row's `vendor_key` — `O111`'s grouping key, which strips legal suffixes, trailing periods and "&" — and read it with **`String(c.name).toLowerCase()`**. So **"Hill Country Milling Co." looked up `hill country milling co.` against rows filed under `hill country milling` and found NOTHING.** Two of four real Red River vendor names disagree; measured, not guessed.
  - **★★★ AND THE FAILURE DIRECTION IS THE ONE THAT COSTS: A MISSING 1099, SILENTLY.** Zero payments → under the $600 floor → *"nothing to file"* for a supplier who is owed one, **and there is no screen on which that absence looks like anything.** A wrong 1099 is at least visible on the form.
  - **★★★ `C256`'S OWN NOTE DESCRIBES THIS EXACT FAILURE — *"otherwise their payments split and both halves fall under the threshold, which is a wrong answer that looks tidy"* — AND IT GOT THE ROW SIDE RIGHT AND THE CONTACT SIDE WRONG.** Knowing the rule is not the same as applying it on both sides of a lookup. **`O125`'s shape a third time: one string doing two jobs.**
  - **★★ THE TEST THAT SHOULD HAVE CAUGHT IT ASSERTED THE EXPRESSION `r.vendor_key || r.vendor` — THE HALF THAT WAS ALREADY RIGHT.** It pinned the mechanism on one side of a boundary and called the contract proved: **·3a's shape, in the guard written specifically to prevent this bug.** Rewritten to run the real `plan1099` over the three real names whose grouping key is not their lower-cased self.
  - **★★★ THE SECOND DEFECT: `contacts.is_1099` IS WRITTEN AND READ BY NOTHING.** `C256` replaced the flag with a derivation and left the writer behind — the Tax page, `form1099.js` and the AI context all read `is_1099_exempt` and `business_type` instead. **So the badge was a control that changed nothing, on the one subject in this product filed with the IRS under the accountant's name** — and it could show **"1099 ✓"** on a food supplier the Tax page correctly calls *"paid for goods, which aren't reported on a 1099 at any amount"*. **Two screens, opposite answers, same question.** Demonstrated with the real functions before it was called a defect.
  - **★ THE BADGE IS THE DERIVATION NOW, READ-ONLY, THROUGH THE SAME FUNCTION THE TAX PAGE CALLS — so they cannot disagree by construction** — and `setVendor1099` is REMOVED rather than left uncalled (`O96`/`C255`: dead plumbing that looks wired). Its four tests went with it, **replaced by the property that took over: nothing writes this field as a decision.** A test deleted along with its subject is a guarantee lost quietly.
  - **★★★ AND I SHIPPED A SILENT BUG INSIDE THIS FIX: I WROTE `VERDICT.REPORTABLE`, WHICH IS NOT A KEY.** `undefined === "eligible"` is merely false, so the branch never runs, **the badge never appears, the build is clean and the suite is green.** Caught only because a test RAN the badge rather than grepping for it. **New guard: every `VERDICT.*` referenced in `src/` must be a real key**, with an anti-vacuity check so it cannot pass by finding none — the cheap general catch for a class that has no other symptom.

- **★★★ C317 — ONE SUPPLIER, TWO ROWS: THE CONTACT↔LEDGER JOIN WAS THE SAME ASYMMETRY AS `C316`, ONE SCREEN OVER (2026-09-10). FOUR MUTATIONS, FOUR REDS.** Found by sweeping for the class rather than stopping at the instance — §11 already records **three** implementations of vendor identity, and `C316` was a fourth mismatch, so the question was how many more.
  - **★★★ `buildVendorSummary` GROUPS ON `vendor_key` AND LABELS EACH GROUP WITH THE MOST RECENT DISPLAY NAME (`C210`) — SO THE LABEL IS DELIBERATELY UNSTABLE.** A supplier billed as *"Hill Country Milling Co."* in January and *"Hill Country Milling"* in March is ONE group carrying the March spelling. The screens joined a contact to that **by raw lower-cased name**, so the match missed and the Vendors tab rendered the supplier **TWICE: once as a contact with no spend, once as a ledger group with no contact details — with $1,700 on the half that had no name to show.** Demonstrated with the real builder before it was called a defect.
  - **★★ THE INSTABILITY IS WHAT MAKES IT WORSE THAN A MERELY NARROW MATCH: *WHICH* CONTACTS JOIN DEPENDS ON WHICH SPELLING ARRIVED LAST.** A vendor can drop off the join by being invoiced again.
  - **★ THE CUSTOMER SIDE HAD IT TOO**, and worse in one respect: `ledgerCustomers` was a `Set` of raw `i.vendor` strings, so **two spellings became two customers** and each row showed part of what they had been billed. Now one row per key, labelled by the most recent spelling — the same rule the vendor list uses.
  - **★★★ AND THE SUBTLE PART, WHICH I ALMOST GOT WRONG: `buildVendorSummary` APPLIES THE `O111` ALIAS INDEX BEFORE GROUPING.** A join keyed on `vendorGroupKey` alone would have missed **exactly the vendors a person had taken the trouble to merge by hand** — the most visible possible way to be wrong. Both sides now go through `applyAlias`, and the summary's OWN `key` is used rather than re-derived from its label. **A test pins the assumption the whole thing rests on** — that `vendorGroupKey(name)` and `entityKeyFor(name)`, which the alias index uses, agree for a bare contact name.
  - **★ AND `ledgerVendors` WAS DECLARED, FILTERED AND READ BY NOTHING.** Removed rather than repaired (`O96`/`C255`) — I was three lines into making dead code alias-aware before checking whether anything called it.
  - **★★ THE SWEEP'S OTHER FINDING IS A NON-FINDING, AND IT IS WORTH RECORDING AS ONE: ~24 MORE SITES COMPARE VENDOR NAMES BY RAW LOWER-CASE, AND THEY ARE **SYMMETRIC** — both sides raw, so they under-match rather than mismatch.** Chiefly `rules.find(r => r.vendor?.toLowerCase() === inv.vendor?.toLowerCase())` in seven places: a vendor rule written as *"Hill Country Milling"* does not fire on *"Hill Country Milling Co."*. **That is a real narrowness and it is NOT this commit's to change** — moving rule matching onto the canonical key is a WIDENING, and a widening of vendor identity is the one-way door `C202`/`C280` exist to guard. **Recorded, not silently converted.**

- **★★★ C318 — THE BULK-REMOVAL BUTTON WAS BUILT AND SHIPPED INTO THE WRONG BRANCH (2026-09-10). FIVE MUTATIONS, FIVE REDS.** Found by continuing the *"what can a client actually DO on these screens"* audit into the write controls, and reading the JSX structure rather than the diff.
  - **★★★ THE ACTION BAR LIVED INSIDE `{filter==="contracts" && …}` AND THE CHECKBOXES THAT FILL IT ARE IN THE MAIN TRANSACTIONS TABLE.** Brace-matched: the contracts block ran **158–208**, the bar sat at **165**, the checkboxes at **220 and 263**. **So you could tick transactions and there was NO BUTTON, and the button rendered only where there is nothing to tick.**
  - **★★★ AND §11 RECORDS WHAT THAT COST: remediating the O83 double-book took SCRIPTED DATABASE ACCESS to remove 14 entries, because the app "could only do one at a time".** `C227`'s commit message says *"bulk removal finally has a button"* — it built the button and put it somewhere it can never be pressed. **The capability has been one branch away from working ever since.**
  - **★★ IT WAS INVISIBLE FOR THE `C195(7)` REASON, WHICH IS WHY NOBODY CAUGHT IT: IN THE CONTRACTS VIEW `picked` IS ALWAYS EMPTY, SO THE BAR NEVER DREW, SO NOTHING EVER LOOKED BROKEN.** A control whose input is always empty is indistinguishable from one with nothing to do — recorded as a lesson in 2026-08-08 and reproduced here by a different route.
  - **★ AND THERE WAS A REACHABLE, WORSE PATH: `picked` IS COMPONENT STATE AND SURVIVED A FILTER CHANGE.** Ticking three rows and switching to Contracts **did** draw the bar — *"3 selected"*, over a table those three rows are not in. Now cleared when the filter changes: **a selection is about the rows you were looking at.**
  - **★★★ AND THE FIX CRASHED THE SCREEN ON MOUNT BEFORE IT WORKED — CAUGHT BY TWO GUARDS, NOT BY ME.** My `useEffect(…, [filter])` went in above `const filter`, a temporal-dead-zone reference; `noTdzInHookDeps` and `C246`'s render sweep both went red immediately. **Second TDZ of the day** — `navSeat` reached for `view` the same way in `C312`. **Both times the guard was the thing that noticed**, which is the argument for the render sweep existing at all: a screen that throws on open is invisible to every logic test in the suite.
  - **★ THE MOVE IS PINNED STRUCTURALLY, BECAUSE A STRUCTURAL READ IS WHAT FOUND IT.** A unit test cannot click; it CAN brace-match the two branches and assert the bar and every checkbox are inside the same one — **which is the entire content of the bug** — and separately that the planner, the signed-month refusal and the confirmation still stand between a selection and a write.

- **★★★ C319 — THE RENDER SWEEP NOW RUNS WITH ROWS IN THE COMPANY, AND IN BOTH SEATS (2026-09-10). SEVEN MUTATIONS; TWO SURVIVED AND BOTH SURVIVALS WERE THE POINT.**
  - **★★★ `C246`'s HARNESS HANDS EVERY UNKNOWN KEY AN EMPTY ARRAY, SO NO ROW HAD EVER RENDERED.** It caught two crash-on-mount bugs the day it shipped — including one of mine this morning — and it left **the majority of every screen unexecuted**: row bodies, joins, per-row conditionals. `C246` named that limit in its own comment; this is the other half of it. The fixture is **small and derived** — only the collections that drive rows, with `vendorSummary` built by the REAL builder — because the harness is right that a full state mirror would be stale in a week.
  - **★★★ AND THE FIRST VERSION DIDN'T ACTUALLY REACH THE ROWS. A CRASH PLANTED IN VendorsView's ROW BODY SURVIVED IT.** `vendorsSelectedContact` was absent from the fixture, the Proxy answered `[]`, **`[]` IS TRUTHY**, so `if (selectedContact)` took the DETAIL pane and the list never drew. **A sweep that renders the wrong branch is indistinguishable from one that renders the right one, because both "don't throw"** — `C195(7)`, inside the test harness. Selection keys are explicitly `null` now, and a **positive** check asserts the fixture's data reaches the page for eight screens.
  - **★★ THE SAME `[]`-IS-TRUTHY SHAPE DEFEATED A CORRECT GUARD IN THE APP'S OWN CODE.** TrustPanel writes `acknowledgementFor ? acknowledgementFor(label) : ""` — right in every way — and an empty array sails past the test and then fails to be callable. **Getting the TYPE right is the only fix that is not a trade:** a falsy default would just make views render their no-data branch instead.
  - **★★ WHICH CONTEXT VALUES ARE FUNCTIONS IS NOW READ OUT OF `App.jsx` RATHER THAN GUESSED FROM THE NAME.** A hand-written list of callables across ~390 keys is wrong the moment somebody adds one, **and its incompleteness was invisible while nothing rendered** — `payrollCodes()` and `acknowledgementFor(label)` were never reached, so the gaps looked like nothing. Imported helpers remain a stated gap in the derivation, and the populated sweep is what surfaces them loudly.
  - **★★ BOTH SEATS, BECAUSE SINCE `C313`–`C315` THE SEAT DECIDES WHAT A SCREEN RENDERS** — and the fixture had `navSeat` as the STRING `"cockpit"`, stale since `C312` made it an object, so `navSeat.isReviewerSeat` read `undefined` and **every screen was being rendered in its CLIENT branch while the fixture claimed the cockpit.** A planted client-only crash now dies.
  - **★★★ AND THE SECOND SURVIVING MUTATION CORRECTED MY OWN CLAIM, WHICH IS WHY IT IS RECORDED RATHER THAN QUIETLY FIXED.** I asserted the new check proved `C317`'s contact↔ledger join; breaking that join (`ledger: undefined`) left everything green. **`v.ledger` feeds exactly one thing — a `lastDate` fallback.** The figures on that screen come from `txnsForVendor`, `C317`'s OTHER half, and that is what the assertions kill. **An assertion aimed at the wrong half of a fix is the ·3a shape, and I wrote one before checking which half carried the value.**
  - **★ THE DUPLICATE-ROW CHECK IS SEPARATE FROM THE TOTALS CHECK ON PURPOSE:** `C317`'s symptom was one supplier rendering TWICE, and **each of those two rows had a correct sum of its own** — so a totals assertion alone would have passed straight through it. Counting the occurrences is what catches the split.

- **★★★ C320 — ONE SIDEBAR, AND THE CPA IS THE OWNER PLUS A REVIEW LAYER (2026-09-11). FIVE MUTATIONS, FIVE REDS.** Operator: *"I don't think the two views should really be too different. The owner needs to see all the info they need — we're pretty much there. We don't need all these tabs in the other view. The full view for the CPA is more just an over-the-top review."*
  - **★★★ TWO SEATS HAD BECOME TWO PRODUCTS.** `C312` gave the CPA a fourteen-row grouped cockpit; `C313`/`C314` gave the owner eight plain rows. Two navs, two vocabularies, for one company's books. **The owner's eight rows ARE the product; the CPA's extra is a REVIEW LAYER — the queue, the sign-off, the exception tools — not a parallel set of destinations.** Everyone gets the same eight rows; a reviewer gets exactly one more, *Review*.
  - **★★ THE WORKBENCH IS NOT GONE — IT MOVED OFF THE SIDEBAR AND BEHIND THE REVIEW LAYER.** *"We don't need all these tabs"* is about TABS. Bank Import, Reconcile, Matching and Payroll stay openable by a reviewer (the route guard is unchanged), but they are reached FROM Review — a tool strip on that screen, rendered from one `REVIEW_TOOLS` list — and from the exception cards and Home's routing. **A tab that exists so a CPA can wander to it is the QBO-bookkeeper IA the North Star names; a tool you reach from the card that needs it is the review layer working.** Standing on any of the four lights the Review row, so a CPA on Reconcile sees where they are.
  - **★★★ AND REMOVING SEVEN ROWS AT ONCE IS EXACTLY HOW MATCHING HAPPENED SEVEN TIMES — so `C312`'s lesson is a RULE now: every screen that lost its row must have a door somewhere other than the sidebar it just left.** The rule ran, and found **Payroll and Send Invoice had none.** The tool strip is Payroll's door; a *"Send an invoice →"* button on *Money owed to you* is Send Invoice's — billing a customer belongs on the screen about what customers owe. **And the test pins the fact its first run found: Payroll's ONLY door is the strip**, so if the strip ever goes it fails on Payroll by name.
  - **★★ ONE VOCABULARY, REVERSING `C314` ON THE OPERATOR'S CALL.** `C314` gave the CPA "Payables"/"Receivables" and pinned that they KEEP the accounting words. With one nav there is one label set, and it is the plain one — **§9 exempts reviewer copy from the jargon bar; it never required jargon**, and a CPA reading *"Bills to pay"* loses nothing.     | Lone Star Restaurant Supply | `lone star restaurant supply` | `lone star restaurant supply` | ✅ |

An **unrecognised source is EXCLUDED and counted, never guessed** — a wrong key merges two vendors and launders one's mapping onto the other's charges, a missing key books to suspense and flags, and those two errors are not symmetric. `identity_source: 'resolved' | 'read'` is carried per observation and surfaced as `identityMix` **in the report shape**, per the operator's instruction that a PROCEED resting on READ identities must not be able to hide.

- **★★ C201 — IDENTITY RESOLUTION NEEDS A PER-SOURCE STRATEGY (2026-08-25). The one-rule split is CONFIRMED BROKEN on two of four sources; `065` is blocked until this settles.**
  - **★ THE DEFECT, REPRODUCED IN CODE.** Payroll descriptions read `Gusto Payroll — 2026-02-28 – 2026-03-13`: an **EM-dash** after the vendor, an **EN-dash** between the period dates. `rawDescriptorOf` splits on the first `" – "` (en-dash) and therefore splits at the DATE RANGE — `rawDescriptorOf(...)` → `"2026-03-13"`, `entityKeyFor("2026-03-13")` → `null`. **Every payroll line resolves to nothing.** *(One correction to the report: it lands in `skipped.no_identity`, not `skipped.ambiguous_descriptor` — the split SUCCEEDS and yields a date. It is counted, so not silent, but it is indistinguishable from a genuine trace-number row.)* It has never fired in anger only because the backfill query filtered `source='bank_import'`; the unfiltered re-pull would have hit it immediately.
  - **★★ ROOT CAUSE — THERE ARE TWO DESCRIPTION-CONSTRUCTION PATHS, AND NEITHER KNOWS ABOUT THE OTHER.** `persistJournalEntry` (App.jsx:1386) builds `` `${invoice.vendor} – ${invoice.description}` `` — en-dash, vendor on the LEFT. `persistMultiLineEntry` (App.jsx:1482) takes `entry.description` **verbatim**, so payroll keeps whatever `payrollEntryForImport` made. And `payments.js:70` returns `` `Payment – ${vendor}` `` — a description that **already contains the separator**, which is why manual entries come out as `Vendor – Payment – Vendor` with three parts. The descriptor is a DISPLAY STRING assembled from structured data that already existed, and identity resolution has been trying to reverse-engineer it. **That is the actual defect; the em-dash is a symptom.**
  - **★ THE DECISIVE REFRAME: only ONE source has a descriptor problem at all.** Rail-stripping exists because bank descriptors are noisy. The other three sources carry a vendor as STRUCTURED DATA and never had variants to resolve — so for them the open-book objection does not apply, because there is nothing to resolve. Reading a field is not cheating on a test the field was never sitting for.

    | source | description shape | vendor identity | strategy |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `bank_import` | `Vendor – RAW BANK TEXT` | RIGHT half, genuinely noisy | **RESOLVE** (rail-strip + normalize) |
    | `payroll` | `Gusto Payroll — start – end` | none on the right; it is not a vendor mapping at all | **EXCLUDE** |
    | `universal_upload` | `Vendor. – PRODUCT DESCRIPTION` | LEFT half; right is a product | **READ** (normalize only) |
    | `manual` | `Vendor – Payment – Vendor` | LEFT half; right is a synthetic phrase | **READ** (normalize only) |

  - **★ PAYROLL SHOULD BE EXCLUDED FROM `vendor_state` ENTIRELY, not parsed.** A payroll register books to salaries / payroll tax / cash / taxes-payable by STRUCTURE, not by a vendor→account judgement — there is no mapping for the ladder to learn. **Amendment A §2 already excludes payroll register entries from shadow scoring**, so excluding them here makes the backfill agree with the criterion instead of quietly disagreeing with it.
  - **★★ AND THE GUSTO "BAND EVENT" IS A UNITS MISMATCH, NOT A SPEND CHANGE — this is why excluding payroll matters.** Gusto reads **3,150.00** via `bank_import` in Jan/Feb and **4,000.00 + 306.00** via `payroll` from March. Those are not the same quantity: **3,150 is NET pay** (gross 4,000 less 850 withholdings) and **4,000 is GROSS**. A unified entity would flag a 1.27× band event and the flag would be **wrong** — the spend did not change, the measurement did. A vendor whose amounts arrive from two different paths measuring two different things cannot have a meaningful band, which is a second, independent reason payroll does not belong in `vendor_state`.
  - **DORMANCY — one artefact, two real.** GUSTO was a query artefact, confirmed: it continues biweekly March–July under `source='payroll'`, hidden by the `bank_import` filter. **HARTLINE and the BANK MONTHLY SERVICE FEE are REAL** — a query across all three names returns nothing matching them after 2026-03-01. They genuinely stop in February, so **dormancy-on-arrival for those two is correct behaviour**, and the decay is the state machine working rather than a bug.
  - **▶ HOLD — `065`/the backfill does not proceed until the per-source strategy is settled.** A backfill built on a rule that fails on two of four sources would seed exactly the wrong entity keys, and a state machine seeded with wrong keys is inert on arrival and green.
- **★★ C201 — THE GRADUATION PREVIEW RAN (2026-08-24). 70 rows · 7 deleted excluded · 63 observations · 13 entities · 7 would graduate.** Four findings, and the first is the one the whole calibration exists for.
  - **★★ CULINARY EDGE GRADUATES TO KNOWN — the specimen that founded the familiarity axis, promoted by the migration built to prevent it.** Two auto-bookings of $850.00 (04-14, 05-14), two signed months, agreeing mapping, identical amounts: it clears Q1 exactly. Specimen 3 is the never-seen vendor auto-booked at confidence 88 on name plausibility into the WRONG LEDGER CLASS — the program's only wrong-class entry. It would have arrived as a clean row in a green preview. **→ AMENDMENT B** (`docs/CALIBRATION_SPEC_O88_AMENDMENT_B.md`, drafted, unsigned): backfill graduation additionally requires ≥1 EXPLICITLY attested observation. Live Q1 unchanged.
    - **★ AND THE AMENDMENT DOES NOT DISCRIMINATE — it defaults. Say so.** `exception_resolved` is **null on all 63 live rows** and `recoded` cannot be derived at all, because `persistRecode` updates `account_id` IN PLACE and leaves no marker (O108). So **zero explicit observations exist** and the rule graduates **0 of 13**. It is adopted as a rule because it stays correct if the data ever becomes discriminating; it must never be REPORTED as a test it cannot perform. Every vendor starts STRANGER and re-earns KNOWN over two live months — Bluebonnet costs 2 cards instead of 7.
  - **★ TOAST SHOULD GRADUATE AND CANNOT — the exact inverse of Culinary Edge.** Three observations, three distinct months, ONE agreeing account (`a3edd514`) — a textbook KNOWN vendor. It splits into **three STRANGERs** because the MONTH NAME is embedded in the descriptor: `…MERCHANT FEES JAN` / `…FEB` / `TOAST MERCHANT FEES APRIL` normalise to three different keys. **DECIDED 2026-08-24: leave it split. NO month-name stripping** — word-stripping is precisely the anti-merge risk the narrow-strip design exists to avoid. **Toast is instead the motivating case for the UNIVERSAL tier**: a national payment processor belongs in the curated directory with a canonical mapping, which resolves identity by RECOGNITION rather than string surgery. **Carried into C202's directory seed as a named test case.** If it still splits once the directory lands, a tested month-name rule gets revisited then.
    - **BAND NOTE, corrected by computation rather than intuition.** A band built from all three amounts (214.36 / 228.91 / 487.20) flags NONE of them — the outlier inflates σ enough to swallow itself (mean 310.16, ±2σ → 3.16–617.15). But the band that would actually exist when April arrives is built from Jan+Feb only: mean 221.64, σ 10.29, band 201.06–242.21 — and **April's 487.20 lands OUTSIDE at 2.2× the pattern.** The band is built from attested history AT THE TIME, never retrospectively, and that is what makes it work. Worth watching in shadow.
  - **★ IDENTITY RESOLUTION WAS EXERCISED AFTER ALL — and the earlier claim that the corpus is flat needs correcting.** GUSTO collapsed FOUR distinct raw strings (`GUSTO PAYROLL 011526/013026/021326/022726`) into one entity — the trailing-numeric tail strip working exactly as designed. So the resolver was tested on real data, **passed on Gusto and failed on Toast**. Lone Star is the constant one; the corpus is not uniformly flat. *(The C200 corpus guard still stands: the four Lone Star VARIANTS remain absent, so Specimen 2's flapping cause is still unrepresented.)*
  - **★ THE RIGHT-HALF RULE EARNED ITSELF ON REAL DATA.** The bank fee arrived under two DIFFERENT resolved names — `Bank` and `Bank (Monthly Service Fee)` — with identical raw text (`MONTHLY SERVICE FEE`). Resolving from the full string would have split one vendor into two. The operator's finding, vindicated on the first run.
  - **DORMANCY DECAY IS PROBABLY A QUERY ARTEFACT, NOT A FINDING — not acted on.** Gusto, Hartline and the bank fee graduate then immediately decay (unseen since 2026-02, `asOf` 2026-08). The extraction filtered `source='bank_import' AND category='Expenses'`, so payroll booked through the REGISTER path and anything hitting a non-expense account is invisible. Gusto vanishing after February is payroll moving paths, not a restaurant that stopped paying staff; a monthly bank fee ending in February is likewise implausible. **A re-pull without those two filters is owed before the planner is touched.**
- **★★ C201 — TWO FINDINGS FROM THE BACKFILL EXTRACTION (2026-08-23), both about the EVIDENCE rather than the code.**
  - **★ THE OPEN-BOOK PROBLEM.** `journal_entries.description` on a bank line is **not** the raw bank descriptor — it is `"Resolved Vendor Name – RAW BANK TEXT"`, en-dash separated (`ledger.js:31` splits the same way for display). Resolving identity from the FULL string would have graded the resolver against a string **containing its own answer**: every variant resolves perfectly because the resolved name is sitting in the input, and the backfill reports flawless grouping while proving nothing about the function under test. An open-book exam scored as closed-book. **Caught by the operator before the planner ran.** Fixed: `rawDescriptorOf` takes the RIGHT half only, and a row with no separator is **excluded and counted** (`skipped.ambiguous_descriptor`) rather than silently scored on the contaminated string — falling back to the whole string is the contaminated case in disguise. Two tests pin it from both sides: same raw text under DIFFERENT resolved names must group as ONE vendor; one resolved name over two different raw texts must stay TWO.
  - **★★ THE FIXTURE PROGRAM CANNOT EXERCISE IDENTITY RESOLUTION.** Lone Star's raw bank text is **byte-identical across all four months** (`ACH DEBIT - LONE STAR RESTAURANT SUPPLY`). **Specimen 2's flapping cause — descriptor variance — is not represented in the drive data at all.** The four variants the spec names as the seed corpus were never in the ledger; they were in the statements, or in the reading of them. **Normalisation has no work to do on a constant string, so a clean backfill preview over this data is an artefact of the fixture, not evidence about the resolver.** `planVendorBackfill` now reports `variance.identityResolutionUnexercised` so a green result can never be read as a tested one, and the C200 corpus guard records that the fixtures cannot supply what it has been waiting for. **OWED: the real statement strings, or explicitly-labelled synthetic variants.**
  - **JANUARY DELETED TWINS — re-import residue, correctly excluded.** Every January line has a deleted twin (same descriptor, amount and date; one soft-deleted, one not). The planner drops deleted lines before anything else and counts them; a test holds it. Without that, every January vendor would have **doubled its observation count** — and since Q1 needs two observations, **a one-month vendor would have graduated on a single month's re-import.** Q1's distinct-months rule would have caught it anyway, which is a second reason that rule is stored as `text[]` rather than a count.
- **★★★ C264 — `O120`: WHICH QUESTIONS DESERVE TO STOP YOU (2026-08-30). ▶ THE HOLD SAID "no design"; THE CALL IS MADE HERE AND SAID SO.**
  - **The old banner read *"⚠ N invoices need your input before booking — scroll down to review"*: a bare count and an instruction to scroll.** Ten cards sat unanswered through the August drive while the header said the books were correct.
  - **★★★ THE QUESTION WAS NEVER "MODAL OR NOT", IT IS *WHICH CARDS EARN AN INTERRUPTION* — and the discriminator was already recorded: a card whose wrong answer books SILENTLY wrong may stop you; one whose wrong answer is visible and undoable waits its turn.** The `O114` lifecycle card is the archetype of the first — *"different purchase"* mints a phantom payable, *"same purchase"* suppresses a real charge, **and the more dangerous of the two leaves nothing on any screen.** A low-confidence category question is the archetype of the second: wrong is visible in the account and a recode undoes it.
  - **★★ AND IT IS ORDERING, NOT INTERRUPTING. NOTHING OPENS A MODAL — a test asserts the module contains no modal, dialog or confirm at all.** A pop-up per card **turns a sitting into a gauntlet**, which is the opposite of *"book everything and batch the judgement to close"*. The dangerous ones simply come FIRST and are counted by name, so **they cannot be buried behind the harmless ones. "Louder" was never the fix.**
  - **★★★ AN UNCLASSIFIED CARD *WAITS*, AND THE DEFAULT DIRECTION IS THE WHOLE SAFETY OF THIS.** Drifting into "stops" rebuilds the gauntlet the batch exists to avoid, and an unknown card is far likelier to be an ordinary category question. **Adding a kind to the stopping set is a decision someone makes; arriving there is not.**
  - **★ KEYED ON WHAT A WRONG ANSWER *DOES*, NOT ON SEVERITY OR AMOUNT.** A $40 lifecycle card stops you and a $40,000 category question waits — **the question is whether you could TELL, not how much is at stake.**
  - **★ THE BANNER NAMES THE KINDS**, because a bare count makes ten harmless questions look like ten problems, **which is how a person learns to ignore the number entirely** — and when nothing is dangerous it no longer sounds like an alarm.
  - **★ FIFTH COMMENT-GUARD FALSE POSITIVE:** the no-modal test matched the file's own explanation of why there is no modal. Comments stripped, as every previous instance ended up doing.

- **★★ C263 — REUSING WHAT WE ALREADY READ FROM A DOCUMENT (2026-08-30). `O113` proposal 3, accepted 2026-08-26. **Migration `084` APPLIED & VERIFIED LIVE 2026-08-30 — two checks, two passes:** both columns present · **91 documents, 0 carrying a stored reading** (additive as designed — **a stored answer nobody produced is an answer nobody can trust**, so a non-zero count would have been a FAILURE rather than a head start) · the partial index is on `(company_id, content_hash) WHERE content_hash IS NOT NULL`, the same pair C193's dedupe already uses. Ledger: through `061`, plus `063`, `064`, `066`, `068`–`084`.**
  - Identical bytes re-uploaded re-ran the whole pipeline. **C193 already hashes every document to dedupe the library, so the key costs nothing new** — and the August drive re-uploaded twice, so this is a path people take rather than a hypothetical.
  - **★★★ THE WHOLE DESIGN IS IN *WHICH* ANSWERS MAY BE REUSED, NOT IN THE REUSING.** What KIND of document it is, and what was ON it, are **properties of the bytes** — safe. **WHICH ACCOUNT it belongs to is a property of the COMPANY'S CHART, which changes** (two migrations added accounts today, learned mappings move, accounts get renamed). **Caching it would pin a booking decision to a chart that no longer exists — the same document re-uploaded after a chart change would book to yesterday's answer, silently, and look identical to a correct one.** Never cached, and `extractionToStore` **drops a coding if it is handed one**, so the omission is structural rather than a convention a caller can forget.
  - **★★ `EXTRACTION_VERSION` IS WHAT STOPS THE CACHE OUTLIVING THE THING IT CACHED.** If the extraction prompt changes, every stored answer came from a model that no longer exists here — **reusing it would make a prompt fix invisible on exactly the documents most likely to be re-uploaded.** It is a **hand-set constant, deliberately not derived**: a hash of the prompt file would invalidate on a typo fix and keep on a semantic change, and **only a person can tell those apart.**
  - **★ AN ENTRY THAT CANNOT ANSWER IS A MISS, NOT A HIT.** An empty extraction would otherwise short-circuit into *"we read this and found nothing"* — the `O98` shape with an AI call's cost saved and the answer thrown away. **Each miss names its kind** (`none` / `stale_version` / `unreadable` / `empty`), so *"the cache never hits"* has somewhere to look.
  - **★ AND A REUSE IS SAID OUT LOUD**, because a re-upload that finishes instantly and silently looks like it did nothing at all — the `O128` shape, where the best available outcome read as a null result.

- **★★★ C261 — THE HIGHEST-VOLUME BOOKING PATH WAS NOT IN THE GAAP GUARDRAIL (2026-08-30).**
  - **§12 says every economic event gets a pure builder with a test asserting exact Dr/Cr, validated against `gaapInvariants`. `buildBankLineEntry` had the builder and not the validation.** It was tested for its SHAPE in five files while **no test ever ran it through Dr=Cr, the accounting equation, or the net-income rules** — and **statements are how most transactions enter these books.** Every drive to date has been statement-shaped. **A builder that produces most of the ledger belongs in the file that checks the ledger.** Both directions added (money out, money in), expanded the same way `persistJournalEntry` posts them so the invariants run on **what actually reaches the books**.
  - **★★ AND THE INTERESTING PART IS A HAZARD THAT IS CURRENTLY HARMLESS.** The builder decides debit-or-credit from **`txn.type === "revenue"`** — the denormalized-flag derivation §9 names as the root cause of the whole `O73` bank-matching class. **It is safe today ONLY BY VIRTUE OF SOMETHING UPSTREAM:** `bank_statement_lines` has a `direction` column and **no `type` column**, so the pipeline's `l.type || (l.direction === "in" ? …)` always falls through to the statement's own answer.
  - **★★★ "CORRECT BECAUSE OF A SCHEMA NOBODY IS THINKING ABOUT" IS THE SAME SHAPE AS THE REVERSAL GUARD THAT WORKED ONLY BECAUSE A QUERY UPSTREAM FILTERED DELETED ROWS.** So the invariant is pinned directly: **the direction the books get is the direction the STATEMENT reported**, and a test reads `058` and fails if a `type` column ever appears on those lines. **If that day comes, the flag would take precedence over the statement and book money the wrong way round — silently.**
  - **★ AND THE NO-DIRECTION DEFAULT IS PINNED AS "EXPENSE", WITH THE REASON: booking an unknown line as an expense understates income and surfaces in review; booking it as revenue inflates income and looks like good news, which is the direction nobody checks.**

- **★★★ C260 — `O109`: THE ASSISTANT MAY RECOGNISE AN ACCOUNT, NEVER INVENT ONE (2026-08-30).**
  - **`resolveAccountId` IS REACHED FROM THE AI ACTION PATH** (`add_rule` / `set_contact_rule` / `add_recurring`), so **a number the model produced could mint a PERMANENT account on a client's chart** — and the client asked a question in chat, not for their chart to be reorganised. It created whatever it was handed: **audibly in the audit log, silently on screen.**
  - **★★ THE SPLIT IS BETWEEN RECOGNISING AND INVENTING, AND "ALWAYS REFUSE" WOULD HAVE BEEN WRONG.** A code in the canonical chart that this company merely **predates** is recognisable — **exactly what the `O35` audit found for merchant fees on eight of eleven companies.** Refusing it would break *"make a rule for Marketing"* on a company lacking `6300`: **real work, refused for a bookkeeping reason the person cannot see.** A code in NEITHER chart is a number produced from nothing, and **there is no reading under which inventing a general-ledger account on someone's books is the right answer to a chat message.** That one is refused.
  - **★ THE RECOGNISABLE CASE IS NOW SAID OUT LOUD** — *"Added 6520 Merchant Processing Fees to your chart of accounts — you didn't have one."* §9: **an action whose effect is invisible will be repeated**, and a person who cannot see that their chart changed has no way to disagree with it. The `account_materialized` audit action is **kept unchanged**, because the `O108` detector reads it and renaming it would blind an instrument that took a migration and a live diagnosis to restore.
  - **★ THE REFUSAL NAMES THE TWO THINGS THEY CAN ACTUALLY DO** — add it in Settings, or name an existing account. *"I won't do that"* with no way forward is a dead end, and this is a chat surface where the next thing they type depends on what we just said.
  - **★★ AND A TEST OF MINE FAILED ON CORRECT CODE BECAUSE ITS WINDOW WAS A CHARACTER COUNT.** It sliced 400 characters looking for a `return null` that sits ~450 in, behind two long strings. **A count is a guess about how long code is; the closing brace is where the block actually ends.** Sixth mechanical slip of the day, same family, caught the same way.

- **★★ C257 — `O35`: THE PER-COMPANY ROLE AUDIT (2026-08-30). `supabase/verify/O35_role_audit.sql`. A REPORT, AND DELIBERATELY NOT A FIX.**
  - **THE DEFECT IS THAT AN ACCOUNT CAN BE ON THE BALANCE SHEET AND INVISIBLE TO THE CODE.** Companies were seeded by different chart versions, every role-resolved feature looks up `system_role`, and a v1-era account has NULL there. It surfaced through payroll first, and **fixing payroll's roles alone did not close it** — which is why the item asks for an audit across ALL roles.
  - **★★ THE SPLIT BETWEEN SAFE AND UNSAFE IS THE POINT.** Setting a role on an existing account is safe: nothing moves. **Renumbering a code is not** — journal lines point at accounts, and moving a code without re-pointing them **silently re-files history**. The two must never be done by the same script, so the audit lists them separately, proposes neither, and **a test asserts the file contains no `update`, `insert`, `delete` or `alter` at all.**
  - **★ THE SAFE LIST EXCLUDES `O110`'s 36 FOREIGN-CHART ACCOUNTS.** Giving them our roles would collide — **two accounts claiming `utilities` on one company, with `byRole` silently keeping whichever it saw last.** A booking hazard introduced to tidy a report.
  - **★★★ AND THE AUDIT CARRIES A COPY OF THE 59-ROLE CANONICAL LIST, WHICH IS THE FAILURE MODE THIS FILE KEEPS PAYING FOR — a printed census cannot be wrong, only old.** So it is GENERATED from `constants.js` and a test regenerates it and compares, in both directions: **a role added to the chart and not to the audit fails**, and so does **a role in the audit that the chart does not have** (which would report false gaps on every company). Without that, adding a role would quietly stop the audit asking about it, and **every company would look complete.**

- **★★★ C256 — 1099 ELIGIBILITY IS DERIVED NOW, NOT TICKED (2026-08-30). `src/lib/form1099.js`. **WIRED THE SAME DAY, DELIBERATELY** — a careful derivation nothing consults is exactly the `O95` shape this file spends pages on, and *"logic only, a screen later"* is how a field with no reader gets written.**
  - **★★ THE TAX PAGE NOW COUNTS FROM THE DERIVATION, and the old flag count is GONE rather than merely unused.** Payments are grouped on the **same key the vendor list uses (`O111`)**, so a supplier known by two names is one supplier here too — otherwise their payments split and **both halves fall under the threshold, which is a wrong answer that looks tidy.**
  - **★ AND AN EXISTING TEST REQUIRED THE TAX PAGE TO READ THE FLAG.** It pinned the `is1099` / `is_1099` naming fix using *"TaxView mentions `is1099`"* as its evidence — so **not reading the flag at all failed it.** Rewritten to name the property (**no component touches the raw snake_case column**; only the load mapping and the save payload may). **A test that names a reader blocks the reader from improving.**
  - **THE STAKE IS NOT TIDINESS: A 1099 IS FILED WITH THE IRS UNDER THE ACCOUNTANT'S NAME.** The live finding that opened this: on one company **nearly every supplier carried the flag** — food, equipment, a utility — **none of them 1099-NEC reportable**, because the badge was effectively defaulted on. **Wrong flags become wrong filings.**
  - **★★★ THE RULE THE FILE EXISTS FOR: AN UNKNOWN ENTITY TYPE IS NOT "NOT A CORPORATION".** Treating unknown as not-exempt **files 1099s for corporations**; treating it as exempt **misses real ones**. Neither is defensible, so it is neither — `NEEDS_INFO` is a first-class verdict. **`O98` applied where it costs money.**
  - **THREE INPUTS, ALL DERIVED:** *what they were paid* (from the LEDGER, with a correcting credit REDUCING the total, and voided rows not counting) · *what for* (from the ACCOUNT's `system_role`, never the supplier's name — §9's "the account fixes the category") · *what they are* (stated `business_type` first, the name only as weak evidence).
  - **★★ AND THE RULE MOST OFTEN MISSED IS ENCODED: LEGAL AND MEDICAL PAYMENTS ARE REPORTABLE EVEN TO A CORPORATION.** A law firm that is a corporation still gets one.
  - **★ GOODS ARE A DIFFERENT ANSWER FROM "UNDER THE FLOOR", AND IT SAYS WHICH.** One changes next year when they are paid more; the other never does. Reporting them identically is how a supplier gets re-examined every January for no reason.
  - **★★ `utilities` IS DELIBERATELY ABSENT FROM THE ROLE MAP, and a test pins that.** Guessing it is *exactly* how the badge ended up on a utility company. An unclassifiable payment above the floor becomes `NEEDS_INFO` rather than a quiet *"below the floor"* — which would be a claim about a query.
  - **★★ THE HEADLINE COUNTS THE UNSURE ONES TOO.** A proposal reporting only what it is certain of understates the work, and **the unsure ones are precisely the ones a person has to act on.** *(Five mutations, five red — including "treat unknown as reportable", which is the failure mode that costs money.)*

- **★★ C255 — `O129`: THE REPAIR TOOL THAT HAD NO BUTTON (2026-08-30).** `attachDepreciationToExistingAsset` builds a depreciation schedule for equipment already in the books — real, idempotent, owner/admin-gated, **and called by nothing**, because the control it sat behind was removed for a good reason: **it asked you to type a raw journal-entry id, on the page where you read your financial statements.**
  - **★★ THE ANSWER WAS NEITHER "DELETE IT" NOR "PUT THE BOX BACK".** The capability is legitimate — equipment reaches the books through paths that never create a schedule (a QuickBooks import, a hand-entered correction, anything predating the feature) and **without one it sits on the balance sheet at full cost forever.** What was wrong was WHERE it lived. **A repair tool with no button is invisible; a repair tool that asks for a database id is unusable.** It now sits on the entry that needs repairing, where the id is already known and **nobody types anything** — pinned by a test that fails if an id input ever reappears.
  - **★ AND IT OFFERS ITSELF ONLY WHERE IT COULD APPLY: a DEBIT to a fixed-asset account.** A credit is a disposal, and scheduling depreciation against one would be **depreciating a removal**. Anywhere else it would be a control that exists to be declined, which is how a screen teaches you to ignore it.
  - **★ THE VALIDATOR CATCHES SALVAGE ABOVE COST — depreciation that ADDS value** — at the point where the message can be about the number the person typed, rather than in the schedule builder where it would be about an internal total they never see.
  - **★★★ AND A SURVIVING MUTATION TURNED OUT TO INDICT THE CODE, NOT THE TEST.** Deleting an explicit *"did we resolve a fixed-asset account?"* guard changed nothing — an empty set matches nothing, so the membership test already returned false. **The line read as a check and could not affect the outcome: the `if (error)` shape in a different costume.** Removed, with the reasoning kept in both files. **A surviving mutation is a question, not a verdict — sometimes the answer is that the line could never have mattered.**

- **★★ C254 — `O112` WAS NOT A DESIGN QUESTION (2026-08-30).** The item asked: we recognise Toast, Square and Stripe by name, but ten of eleven companies have no card-processing account — **create it, ask first, or leave it parked?**
  - **★★ `6520` AND `6530` ARE ALREADY IN THE DEFAULT CHART.** `068` blessed them after a CPA created them by hand on a real client, so **every company created since gets them** and the ten that don't are simply **older than that decision**. The question is a BACKFILL wearing a design question's clothes.
  - **★★★ AND THE ANSWER TURNS ON *WHEN*, NOT *WHETHER*. Materialising an account MID-BOOKING is the `O108`/`O109` hazard — the system inventing a line on a client's chart while nobody is looking. Adding one as a deliberate, audited SETUP step is what `C223`'s template path already does safely: additive, never renaming, never touching an account that carries transactions. Same account, same code — the difference is entirely that a person is present.**
  - **THEY ARE UNIVERSAL, NOT INDUSTRY-SPECIFIC**, so they apply to every business type **including `Other`** — whose industry list stays deliberately empty. *"A business we cannot name is one whose chart we should not be opinionated about"* is about INDUSTRY opinions; **every business has a bank account.**
  - **★ THE ROLES GO WITH THEM.** A code with no `system_role` is invisible to every `getAccountByRole` lookup (the `O110` finding) — adding the account without the role would have looked like a fix and been one only by coincidence of numbering.
  - **★★ AND AN EXISTING TEST NAMED *"does NOT re-add merchant fees — the generic chart already has them"* NOW READS AS A REJECTION OF THIS AND IS NOT ONE.** It runs against a company holding the full default chart, so the dedupe correctly skips. **Renamed to say "TO A COMPANY THAT ALREADY HAS THEM" and extended to assert the planner DOES add them to a company without them** — the difference is the company, not the rule, and a test whose title states the general case would have blocked this work for the next reader.

- **★★ C253 — `O122`: THE CARD RATE IS MEASURED NOW (2026-08-30). `src/lib/cardRate.js`.**
  - **THE ITEM SAID *"from now on we count them separately every time we test"* AND NOTHING COUNTED ANYTHING.** Every drive and suite here scores INDIVIDUAL behaviours, so **we can end with every behaviour correct and a screen carrying nine cards** — and unit tests structurally cannot catch that: one asserts a card is RIGHT, never that there are few enough of them.
  - **★★★ CATEGORY 1 IS A DEFECT COUNT AND IS NEVER AVERAGED INTO THE RATE.** **A single category-1 card fails the run however small a share of the documents it is.** The test builds a deliberately flattering denominator so the assertion has something to overrule, and **the numbers in it are a FIXTURE, not a measurement** — first written as *"a 1% rate"*, which the operator correctly read as a claim that one in a hundred records fails to save. **It is not true of anything:** `record_failed` has been observed exactly once, firing on **THREE OF THREE** attempts from the `patch`/`values` parameter bug, and never since that fix. The real history is *broken, then fixed* — never a low background rate. **A percentage in an example reads as an observed frequency, and this one did within the hour.** It fails the run because *"a card the user sees every month is a bug wearing a question mark"* and a bug's expected value is zero. **Averaging is how a defect becomes "within tolerance".**
  - **★★ AN UNRECOGNISED CARD IS REPORTED, NEVER DEFAULTED.** Bucketing an unknown kind into "judgement" would flatter the number **by exactly the amount we do not understand** — and a new card kind nobody classified is the most likely place for a category-1 defect to hide. The report says the rate is unreliable while any remain, and **a test asserts every anomaly type and every `ASK_REASON` the product can actually emit is in the taxonomy**, so a new detector cannot arrive unmeasured.
  - **★ THE SPLIT IS THE DELIVERABLE, NOT THE TOTAL, AND A TEST DEMONSTRATES WHY:** a month whose total FALLS while category 2 stays flat is the teaching not sticking — invisible in an aggregate, obvious in the split.
  - **★ ONBOARDING HAS NO TARGET RATHER THAN BORROWING STEADY STATE'S.** Conflating them would either make onboarding look broken or make steady state look fine; *"no target set for this mode yet"* is the honest output.
  - **★★ AND THE TAXONOMY RECORDS WHAT CHANGED CATEGORY.** `duplicate_payment`, `large_transaction`, `round_number` and `missing_recurring` were all category 1 **while a bug was live** — payroll tripping the large-charge detector, staleness measured against wall-clock, duplicates on lifecycle pairs. All four are fixed, all four are now category 3, and **each entry says it used to be a defect**, because a taxonomy that silently re-grades itself hides the improvement it exists to show.

- **★★★ C252 — `O116`: PAYROLL IS HANDLED, NOT HANDED OVER (2026-08-30). A REFACTOR BEFORE IT WAS A FEATURE.**
  - **THE BLOCKER WAS STRUCTURAL, NOT BEHAVIOURAL: the whole payroll pipeline (148 lines) LIVED INSIDE `PayrollView`.** A file dropped on Home could therefore only ever be ROUTED to that screen — and on the **CLIENT seat it was stashed UNPARSED** with *"we've saved that for your accountant"*, so **dropping a register did nothing at all** until somebody else opened a different screen, possibly days later. The home promise is *"drop anything here and we'll handle the rest"*; payroll was the one thing that answered *"go to this other page"*.
  - **★★ MOVED, NOT COPIED — the ·3a rule, applied as the reason the work took this shape.** Two implementations of one contract is how the payroll gate shipped inert: both halves look tested and drift apart. The drop zone, the Home queue and the manual Post button now run **the same code** and cannot disagree about what a register does. A test asserts the view holds **no** poster, gate or request-builder of its own.
  - **★ IT RUNS IN PLACE ON BOTH SEATS BECAUSE THE GATE ALREADY DECIDES THE HARD PART.** A standard register that proves itself POSTS (·3a); anything else is held with the failing reasons attached. So a client gets an OUTCOME — their payroll is in the books — or an honest hold. **THE OTHER THREE STILL ROUTE, DELIBERATELY:** a bank statement opens a reconciliation a person drives, a contract needs terms confirmed, QuickBooks is a mapping exercise. **Payroll is the one whose clean path is fully decided by a gate we already trust.**
  - **★★ THE HELD CASE HAD TO BECOME AN OUTCOME TOO, OR THE FIX WOULD HAVE MADE THINGS WORSE.** The gate refusing is CORRECT for a register that doesn't foot — but without a word, a client who dropped payroll on Home would see the file accepted and then **nothing, forever**, which is precisely what "handled" must not mean. The sentence reads `gate.reasons`, so it cannot describe a hold that did not happen (§9).
  - **★★★ AND SEVEN EXISTING TESTS FAILED, EVERY ONE OF THEM CORRECTLY.** They pin where the payroll pipeline lives, and it moved. **Repointing them was the risky part, and two attempts were wrong in the informative direction:** the *"exactly ONE builder and ONE write call"* guard, naively pointed at `App.jsx`, demanded one `persistMultiLineEntry` in a file that legitimately has eight — **a repointed test that now asserts something else is worse than a failing one** — so it is scoped to the payroll region instead. And `payrollPdf` had to be SPLIT: the pipeline moved, the file PICKER did not, and pointing both at `App.jsx` made the accept-list assertion read a file with no picker in it — **it would have gone green the moment someone deleted the picker entirely.**
  - **★★ ONE TEST WAS REWRITTEN TO NAME ITS PROPERTY RATHER THAN ITS MECHANISM.** `iaCollapse` asserted that bank, payroll and QBO are all STASHED for a client. The property it protects — *a client's file is accounted for and never throws them onto a reviewer screen* — is fully honoured by processing payroll in place; **stashing was one way to achieve it, not the thing itself.** It now also pins that the payroll branch runs BEFORE the seat check, so the client path cannot fall through to a `setView`.

- **★★ C251 — `O111`: TELLING US TWO NAMES ARE ONE SUPPLIER. **Migration `082` APPLIED & VERIFIED LIVE 2026-08-30 — three checks, three passes:** `ARRAY / NO / '{}'::text[]` · **42 contacts, 0 carrying an alias** (additive as designed — a guessed alias is exactly the silent merge the column exists to prevent, so a non-zero count would have been a FAILURE, not a head start) · GIN index present. **Ledger: through `061`, plus `063`, `064`, `066`, `068`–`082`.****
  - **THE CASE, UNCHANGED SINCE C201 FOUND IT:** the bank line reads `ACH DEBIT - FRANKLIN AVE PROPERTIES LP RENT`, the invoice reads `Franklin Ave Properties`. Rail-stripping removes transport noise and legal suffixes, but the descriptor also carries a **PURPOSE word** — `RENT` — saying what the payment was FOR, not who it was TO. One landlord, two vendors.
  - **★★ THE DESIGN DECISION IS THE REFUSAL TO DO STRING SURGERY.** Stripping trailing RENT / FEES / SERVICES would also eat **"Lone Star Restaurant SUPPLY"** and **"Bluebonnet Linen SERVICE"** — and §11 already recorded that reasoning for the Toast month-name split, where the answer was recognition rather than surgery. **A wrong merge is a ONE-WAY DOOR: it launders one vendor's attested mapping onto another's charges, silently.** So the merge is asserted by a person and applied by a **Map lookup** — no scoring, no fuzzy match, no *"did you mean"*. **A test asserts the module contains no similarity code at all**, because once `O106` (suggesting matches) exists the difference between the two is invisible from outside.
  - **★ THREE REFUSALS THE VALIDATOR MAKES, EACH FOR A DIFFERENT DANGER:** a name with **no letters** (the phantom-vendor door, C209) · the contact's **own** name · **a name another supplier already answers to — and it NAMES them**, because *"Lone Star already goes by that name"* is actionable where *"invalid"* is not.
  - **★★ AND A CONFLICT RESOLVES TO NEITHER.** Two contacts claiming one alias leaves the vendor **split — visible** — rather than merged onto whichever row was inserted first. Same rule as the directory's `directoryConflicts()`: **two claimants is worse than no entry.**
  - **STORED ON `contacts`, NOT A NEW TABLE:** a contact IS the vendor record, an alias is another name for that same row, and a join table would invite the question *"which contact does this point at"* that the column answers by construction. Mirrors `companies.aliases` (O75).
  - **★★ IT REACHES BOTH DECISIONS, PINNED BY TESTS (§9 "name the reader"):** the **vendor list** groups Franklin Ave as one row with the totals joined, and the **invoice/payment matcher** now ATTACHES the case that `O114`'s re-drive left open. *(The pre-registered test in `invoicePayment.test.js` used a descriptor WITHOUT the purpose word, so it passed on the string rules alone — the new test uses the real one, where only the asserted alias closes it.)*

- **★★ C250 — THE EMPTY-CATCH SWEEP, AND THE ONE THAT MATTERED MOST WAS A PAIR OF *READS* (2026-08-30).**
  - 23 empty catches wrap a write or an RPC; **most are `localStorage`, Sentry calls, or the notification markers already excused** (a failed marker leaves the badge *visibly* lit). **Three were real.**
  - **★★★ THE TEAM SCREEN FAILED INTO AN EMPTY LIST.** Both queries — members and pending invites — swallowed their error, so a failed load rendered as **"nobody is on this team"** and **"no invites outstanding"** on **the one screen whose entire job is to tell you who can reach your books.** *"We couldn't ask"* and *"there is nobody"* are different answers and **a silently empty list is the most reassuring possible way to be wrong.** This is `O98` on a security surface. It now says the list may be incomplete **and that it is not a confirmation that nobody else has access.**
  - **★★ NO QUICKBOOKS BATCH RECORD MEANS NO UNDO.** The undo works off the `qbo_imports` row, so an import whose batch insert failed was booked and then **could not be reversed as a group** — inside an empty catch, so the person clicking Import learned nothing. It still proceeds (the entries are correct and wanted); what changed is that **the missing safety net is announced BEFORE it is needed**, not discovered when they reach for it.
  - **★★★ AND A TEST I EXPECTED TO PASS FOUND A SECOND ASSET ROLLBACK I HAD NOT FIXED.** Two byte-identical unchecked deletes existed; I hardened one, and the assertion's `indexOf` reached **the other** and failed against what I believed was correct code. **A test failing on correct-looking code is the cheap version of this**; the expensive version is a second copy nobody ever looks at. Both paths now go through **one** checked helper, and the assertion pins that **exactly one** place removes an asset — so a third copy cannot appear either.
  - **★ MECHANICAL SLIP NUMBER FIVE, SAME FAMILY: my slice-end heuristic cut in the wrong place and left orphaned lines**, caught by the build. Bad anchor · reversed slice · no-op mutation · import inside an import · this. **All five are the same failure to verify that an edit did what it was written to do**, and the build or a test caught every one — which is the argument for making the edit and then LOOKING, not for editing more carefully.

- **★★★ C249 — THE UNCHECKED-WRITE CLASS IS CLOSED AS A CLASS (2026-08-30). `tests/uncheckedWrites.test.js`.**
  - **IN ONE DAY THIS SINGLE FACT — PostgREST reports NO error for an update that matched zero rows — PRODUCED:** a delete function whose ids every *"✓ Deleted"* message was built from · **both** Undo buttons · a payment compensation · a recode · the opening-balance supersede · the tax page and settings saves · **and a fix written that same morning carrying the gap it was fixing.** `if (error)` **reads as a check and is not one**, which is why the rule cannot be advisory.
  - **FIVE MORE FIXED FIRST, TRIAGED BY WHETHER A SILENT FAILURE CHANGES A NUMBER OR REPEATS WORK:** the **cutoff date** (§12 load-bearing — no transaction may be dated before it, so a failed save leaves the screen showing one date and every guard enforcing another) · the **bank balance adopted from a statement** · the **business profile** (`fiscal_year_end` drives the retained-earnings split, so a lost write is a wrong FIGURE later, not a missing preference) · **onboarding-complete** (a silent failure greets them again on every load, forever, with nothing they can do) · **`dismissAnomaly`** (`080`'s policy filters a non-reviewer's dismissal out with `USING`, yielding **no error AND no rows** — so the card would have said "dismissed", vanished, and returned on the next load).
  - **★★ AND THE GUARD ALLOWS EXCEPTIONS ONLY WITH A WRITTEN REASON, WHICH IS THE WHOLE DESIGN.** Nine tables are excused and **each names why nobody needs to know it failed** — a failed notification marker leaves the badge *visibly* lit; a failed anomaly transition leaves the note *visibly* open; a lost learning signal is re-taught by the next correction. **A blanket allow-list would be the same as no rule.**
  - **★ A THIRD CHECK REFUSES AN UNUSED EXCUSE.** An excuse for a table nobody writes unchecked any more is **a licence sitting open for the next person who adds one** — the same reasoning that removed the five dead context destructures. Mutation-verified in all three directions: a new unchecked ledger write · a `.select()` removed · an excuse gone stale.

- **★★★ C248 — THE GATE WAS CORRECT AND ITS INPUT WAS NOT (2026-08-30). FIVE LEDGER WRITES.**
