An id that no longer resolves, or a row RLS filters out — **the `C191` id-seam class** — came back as a successful delete.
  - **★★ SO TWO HARDENINGS I VALIDATED, ONE OF THEM THIS MORNING, WERE BUILT ON AN UNCHECKED WRITE ONE LEVEL DOWN.** Same shape as the reversal guard that was only correct by virtue of something upstream: **hardening a gate says nothing about the truthfulness of what it is handed.** The defect was never where either fix was looking.
  - **FOUR MORE, TRIAGED BY STAKES rather than swept blind** (28 unchecked row-targeted writes exist; the other 23 are notification read-markers and similar): **`persistRecode`** — a recode is the operation where *"it didn't throw"* and *"the money moved"* are most easily confused, and it matched on `journal_entry_id` + a debit/credit filter, so `.select("id")` rather than the helper · **the opening-balance supersede** — *"a failure here leaves TWO opening entries"*, the one write in the product whose silent failure **doubles the balance sheet** · **`persistAccountEdit`** — a rename or renumber that saves nothing · **`compensateCapitalization`** — the twin of `C245`'s payment compensation, keeping the books from holding a capitalized asset with no depreciation schedule.
  - **★ AND A MUTATION ESCAPED FOR THE THIRD TIME TODAY ON THE SAME BLIND SPOT: `if (false) { logAudit(…) }` satisfied a test matching the audit NAME.** A source scan cannot see reachability, so the assertion must pin the CONSUMPTION — `if (!r.ok) {` immediately preceding. Three occurrences in one day (contracts Undo · the refused-rows restore · this) is not carelessness, it is a property of source-scanning tests, and the rule is now explicit: **match the branch, never the identifier.**

- **★★ C247 — `O76` HAD NO DETAIL, SO THE CLASS WAS FOUND RATHER THAN LOOKED UP (2026-08-30).** The item read *"screens that don't refresh after a change — fix as a class, not screen by screen"*, one line, no examples. **The class, from the WRITING end: a view writes directly to the database, does not check the write landed, then paints the new value from local state.** The screen is right, the database never agreed, and the *"it didn't refresh"* complaint arrives on the NEXT load when the value silently reverts.
  - **FOUR LIVE INSTANCES, and one is a person's own typing.** **`SettingsView`** reported settings saved while checking only `error` — and then wrote the new name into the header optimistically, so a failed save looked done. **`TaxView`** failed to a `console.warn` and **nothing else**: estimated payments made and which deadlines are filed are **figures a human types**, so a silent failure means they enter their tax position, see no complaint, and find it gone. **A silent failure of the user's own typing is the worst kind — no reason to suspect it, no way to notice.**
  - **★ TWO WERE GENUINELY BEST-EFFORT AND ARE NOW EXPLICITLY SO.** The `aliases` write tolerates a missing column pre-`046`; QBO's batch counters must not fail a completed import. **But "we tolerate this failing" had become "we cannot tell whether it ever saved", which is a different thing** — both now report without blocking. And the counters are not cosmetic: **`imported_count` is what the UNDO reports**, so a lost counter makes the undo say it removed nothing — `C235`'s bug seen from the writing end.
  - **★★★ AND THE FOURTH WAS MY OWN C235 FIX FROM THIS MORNING, CARRYING THE GAP IT WAS FIXING.** C235 hardened the QBO undo to stop claiming success without checking — **and then checked only `error`**, which PostgREST does not set for an update matching zero rows. A batch that failed to be marked undone would still have said *"Import undone ✓"* — **the exact sentence C235 deleted one line above.** Found by a guard written afterwards, not by re-reading it. **The lesson is not "be careful": it is that `if (error)` reads as a check and is not one, which is why the guard has to be mechanical.**

- **★★★ C246 — `O14` CLOSED: SCREENS ARE TESTED NOW, WITH NO NEW DEPENDENCY (2026-08-30). `tests/viewsRender.test.jsx`.**
  - **THE ITEM WAS *"a screen can crash with a green suite"*, AND IT WAS EXACTLY TRUE.** Every test here exercised LOGIC; nothing had ever rendered a view. **Three commits in this session alone renamed or moved context values that views consume** — any of them could have shipped a screen that throws on open against 2,400 passing tests.
  - **★ `renderToString` SHIPS WITH REACT AND NEEDS NO DOM.** No jsdom, no testing-library, no config — the project has deliberately stayed at six dependencies, and the render pass is exactly where a crash-on-open happens. **All 33 views + 3 top-level components render.**
  - **★★ AND THE FIRST HARNESS PRODUCED SEVEN FALSE FAILURES THAT LOOKED LIKE REAL CRASHES.** The verb regex matched `filteredInvoices` ("filter…"), `openingBalances` ("open…") and `signoffs` ("sign…"), handing views a function where they expected an array. **A camelCase verb prefix is only a verb when a capital follows it** — `(?=[A-Z])` is load-bearing and pinned by its own test.
  - **★★★ BUT A MUTATION ESCAPED IT, AND THAT ESCAPE IS THE MORE USEFUL HALF: RENAMING A CONTEXT KEY STILL RENDERED.** The fixture returns an empty array for anything it does not know, so the screen paints — **empty and wrong**. That is the harness being permissive on purpose (a strict fixture would be a second copy of a 394-key state shape, stale in a week), and it means plainly: **THE RENDER SWEEP CATCHES A SCREEN THAT CRASHES, NOT ONE THAT SILENTLY RENDERS EMPTY.**
  - **SO A SECOND CHECK ASSERTS THE CONTRACT, WITH NO FIXTURE AT ALL:** every name a component destructures from `useERP()` must actually be in `erpCtx`. **It immediately found 5 real orphans** — `getOpenAP`, `getOpenAR`, `getUnpaidInvoices`, `getUnpaidReceivables`, `setCustomCOA`, **destructured by 21 views each and existing NOWHERE in `src/`.** Dead copy-paste, harmless today — **and precisely the noise that would hide a real one.** Removed, so the check now has zero exceptions.
  - **★ AND MY PARSER'S FIRST VERSION REPORTED 173 "MISSING KEYS", FIVE OF THEM REAL.** `[\s\S]*?` matched from the first `const {` in `ClarificationFlow.jsx` to a `useERP()` hundreds of lines later, so every local variable, style property and comment word in between became a finding. **A finding list that is 97% noise is worse than no check** — it trains you to skim. Tightened to `[^{}();]*?`: a context destructure is a plain identifier list.

- **★★ C245 — `O17`'s SAFETY NET WAS ITSELF AN UNCHECKED WRITE (2026-08-30).** `markBillPaid` posts the GL movement and THEN flips the paid flag; a compensating reversal removes the movement if the second step fails, **and that reversal is the entire safety property of the two-step design.** It was a bare `.update()` inside a `console.warn` catch — and PostgREST reports no error for an update that matched nothing (C192), **so "it didn't throw" was never evidence it worked.** A silent failure left the books showing cash leaving for a bill still marked unpaid, traced only by a console line nobody reads.
  - **★★★ AND THE COPY MATTERED AS MUCH AS THE WRITE: the generic *"please try again"* invited a SECOND payment movement on top of the orphaned one — the `O123` shape, in money instead of reversals.** A failure that cannot be retried must never be described as one. It now says what is actually true of the books — *"we recorded the money leaving but couldn't finish marking it paid, and couldn't undo it either. **Don't try again** — send this to your accountant"* — and is audited **and** sent to Sentry, because a money-state inconsistency is not a UI event.
  - **▶ THE TWO STEPS REMAIN TWO STEPS.** Making them one is an RPC change (`O95`'s neighbour) and stays open; this removes the way the existing design could fail invisibly.

- **★★ C244 — `O99` CLOSED: NINETEEN HAND-ROLLED AI PARSES, NOW ONE (2026-08-30). And the sweep found two defects the item did not describe.**
  - **`JSON.parse((d.content?.find(b=>b.type==="text")?.text||"{}").replace(/```json|```/g,"").trim())` appeared VERBATIM** across upload, payroll, contracts, QBO, onboarding, screening and reconcile. It breaks the moment the model adds a sentence after the JSON — **which is what killed the payroll parse mid-drive (C188)** — and C188 fixed exactly one copy and listed the rest for "deliberate migration". **`src/` now holds ZERO raw parses of a model reply**, pinned by a test that walks the tree.
  - **★★ THE MIGRATION PRESERVES BOTH OLD BEHAVIOURS EXACTLY, WHICH IS THE WHOLE DESIGN:** *no text at all* → the caller's fallback, as `||"{}"` did (**an absent reply is not a parse failure**, and turning it into one would convert benign no-ops into user-visible errors in a dozen flows at once) · *text that will not parse* → **THROWS**, as `JSON.parse` did, so every existing try/catch still runs (**returning the fallback here would be the silent-failure trade this codebase spends most of its time undoing** — garbage would read as *"nothing found"*). **The only change is that trailing prose parses.** All three pinned, all three mutation-killed.
  - **★★ DEFECT 1, NOT IN THE ITEM — THE CLARIFICATION FLOW'S BRACE REGEX WAS GREEDY.** `/\{[\s\S]*\}/` spans from the FIRST `{` to the **LAST** `}`, so a reply containing two objects produced a string that is not JSON at all — **the account lookup failed on precisely the replies that contained an answer.**
  - **★★ DEFECT 2 — A "RECOVERY" CATCH THAT RE-RAN THE IDENTICAL CALL.** `try { JSON.parse(raw) } catch { try { JSON.parse(raw) } catch { [] } }` — the second attempt is the same call on the same string, so it **could never recover anything the first missed**. It was two ways of writing `catch { [] }` while reading like a fallback strategy. *(Sibling of C195(7): code that cannot do anything, indistinguishable from code with nothing to do.)*
  - **★ AND A SELF-INFLICTED BREAK WORTH RECORDING: my "insert after the last line starting with `import`" heuristic put an import INSIDE a multi-line import** in `ai.js`, producing invalid JS. **Caught by the build, not by a test** — and the check that found the rest was a two-line script asserting no import line follows one ending in `{`. Fourth mechanical slip today (bad anchor · reversed slice · no-op mutation · this): all four are the same failure to verify that an edit did what it was written to do.

- **★★★ C243 — `O95` IS NOW GUARDED AS A CLASS, NOT CHASED AS INSTANCES (2026-08-30). `tests/importMetadataContract.test.js`.**
  - **SIX DEFECTS, ONE FACT.** `post_journal_entry` discards every `p_meta` key outside its six named scalars, and that has produced: **the payroll gate that could never fire** (a whole release) · **payroll bank-line matching, inert** · **depreciation's idempotency marker, inert** · **the reversal marker, inert AND FAILING OPEN — one bill reversed three times** · **the sales-tax control total, INVERTED** · **"the original date is kept on file", kept nowhere and displayed nowhere.**
  - **★★ EVERY ONE WAS FOUND BY A PERSON READING CODE, MONTHS APART. NONE BY A TEST** — because a test that hands the reader its own fixture proves the reader works GIVEN the data and says nothing about whether the data arrives (`·3a`). So the guard asserts the **CONTRACT**: every key read off `import_metadata` must be written by something, the reader roster is pinned by exact equality, nothing may be added to `p_meta` outside the six the RPC keeps, and every metadata write goes through a **checked** update.
  - **▶ WHAT IT CANNOT DO, STATED: it cannot prove a write LANDS at runtime.** Only a live drive does that. It proves nobody has added a SEVENTH reader with no writer — **which is how all six above began.**
  - **★★ AND THE FIRST MUTATION AGAINST IT ESCAPED BECAUSE THE MUTATION NEVER APPLIED.** My `sed` targeted `export function isLiveEntry`; the source says `export const`. The replace was a no-op, the suite stayed green, and **that is indistinguishable from a guard doing its job.** Re-run properly it kills two checks. **A mutation must be confirmed to have CHANGED THE FILE before its survival means anything** — the same shape as a slice whose end anchor precedes its start, hit three times today.

- **★★★ C241 — `O95`'s SALES-TAX INSTANCE IS NOT INERT. IT IS INVERTED (2026-08-30).**
  - **THE MECHANISM IS THE FAMILIAR ONE:** `persistJournalEntry` puts `tax_amount` in `p_meta`, `post_journal_entry` cherry-picks six named scalars and discards the rest, so **`import_metadata.tax_amount` has never once reached the database.** The comment where it is built — *"persisted INDEPENDENT of how it was booked"* — has been describing an intention since the RPC existed.
  - **★★★ BUT THE CONSEQUENCE IS WORSE THAN THE PAYROLL AND DEPRECIATION INSTANCES, WHICH BOTH FAILED CLOSED. `sales_tax_tie` compares *tax the invoices charged* against the Sales-Tax-Payable balance, and with the charged figure stuck at 0 THE NET RUNS BACKWARDS:**
    - **tax booked CORRECTLY** → the liability is credited, charged reads 0 → **MISMATCH → an accuracy flag that BLOCKS SIGN-OFF on books that are right.**
    - **tax MIS-BOOKED INTO REVENUE — the Riverside case the whole net was built for** → liability 0, charged 0 → **TIES, and the one failure it exists to catch passes silently.**
    - **Both proved by running `computeControlTotals` on the two fixtures**, not by reading it. **A guard that fires on correct work and stays quiet on the failure it was written for is worse than an absent guard: the absent one does not train you to ignore it.**
  - **★★ AND WHY A GREEN SUITE NEVER SAID SO — `·3a`'s LESSON, IN THIS CHECK'S OWN TEST FILE.** `tests/controlTotals.test.js` hands its fixtures `import_metadata: { tax_amount: 8 }` **directly**. Reader and fixture therefore agree with each other while neither agrees with the database — **the identical shape that shipped the payroll gate unable to fire for a release.** A test that supplies the field production never writes proves the check works GIVEN the data and says **nothing** about whether the data arrives. **The new suite asserts the WRITE SIDE.**
  - **FIXED with the follow-up `checkedRowUpdate`** ·3c used for payroll and depreciation — **migration-free**, because changing the RPC's contract is its own decision and remains `O95`'s open half.
  - **★★ AND THE SECOND `O95` VICTIM ON THE SAME FUNCTION IS A BROKEN PROMISE TO THE USER, NOT AN INERT FEATURE.** An entry dated into a signed month offers three choices; one is *"record it in the current month instead"*, and the confirmation says **"the original date is kept on file"**. `rebookedIntoOpenMonth` duly builds `original_date` — **onto the invoice object, which `persistJournalEntry` never forwarded into `p_meta` at all, and which the RPC would have discarded had it done so.** The date was kept nowhere and the sentence was a claim about a write that did not happen.
  - **★★★ AND IT HAD NO READER EITHER — `grep` for `original_date` outside the writer returns NOTHING.** So fixing only the write would have produced a field that is stored, correct, and invisible. **Both halves were missing at once**, which is the rare case where "name the reader" and "the write does not land" are the same bug. The panel now renders *"Originally dated 15 Jan — moved here because that month was already signed off"*.
  - **★ `tax_amount` AND `original_date` ARE NOW GONE FROM `p_meta` ENTIRELY, and a test forbids their return.** Leaving them there "for clarity" is exactly how this looked correct for as long as the RPC has existed: **a key in `p_meta` is written nowhere, and the comment describing it becomes a description of an intention.**
  - **★ THE FAILED-STAMP PATH FAILS LOUD, DELIBERATELY.** The entry is correct either way, but a missing stamp makes the control total read 0 against a real liability and **block sign-off with a mismatch nobody can explain** — so it is audited AND said on screen, in terms of what the accountant will see: *"we couldn't record this invoice's sales tax for the monthly cross-check. Your accountant may see a sales-tax mismatch that isn't real."*

- **★★ C240 — THE AUDIT TRAIL WAS RECORDING AN INTENTION AS AN EVENT (2026-08-30). Found by CHECKING TWO LEDGER ITEMS FOR STALENESS, not by looking for a bug.**
  - **BOTH ITEMS WERE INDEED STALE** — *"decide how we identify a vendor for each kind of record"* was settled and shipped in C201 (`identityForEntry`, per-source strategy), and `O124`'s *"'Deleted' still appears even when the delete didn't happen"* was fixed yesterday. **That is now FIVE stale items this week** (O96, the reconcile prefill, the recurring-charge false positives, and these two): the ledger's failure mode is not losing items, it is holding ones that quietly became true.
  - **★★ BUT READING THE CODE TO CONFIRM IT FOUND TWO DEFECTS THE ITEM DID NOT DESCRIBE.** *(1)* **`softDeleteInvoices` wrote its `invoice_deleted` audit rows BEFORE attempting the write**, so a refused delete left *"Deleted: Roma $551.20"* permanently in the audit trail **beside the `invoice_delete_failed` row that contradicts it. The audit trail is the one record an accountant is entitled to trust, and it was being told what we INTENDED.** *(2)* **`allIds.length` is a BATCH-level test**, so five entries with two refused reported *"Deleted 5 entries ✓"* while those two sat removed from the screen and present in the books.
  - **★ THE PARTIAL CASE WAS REACHABLE ONLY FROM THE AI CHAT PATH — the caller with nobody checking its work.** `BooksView` pre-splits signed months through `planEntryRemoval`; `App.jsx:6943` hands over a mixed batch unfiltered. **Yesterday's bulk-delete UI made the batch case ordinary**, which is what moved this from latent to live.
  - **★★ AND THE SAME MUTATION ESCAPED A TEST OF MINE TWICE IN ONE DAY.** Asserting `setInvoices(prev =>` APPEARS survived wrapping it in `if (false)` — identical to the contracts-Undo escape this morning. **A source scan cannot see reachability.** Fixed by pinning what the block BEGINS with, which also kills a reorder. **The general form: assert STRUCTURE or CONSUMPTION, never presence.**

- **★★ C293 — THE GATE THAT MUST NEVER SHOW A FALSE GREEN, SWEPT (2026-08-31).** `C194`: *"Your books match your bank ✓"* rendered UNGATED — a failed completion insert was `console.warn`'d and the success screen appeared anyway, reproduced live with NO row existing. §11 calls that the worst class of bug in this product.
  - **★★★ THE DANGEROUS DIRECTION IS NOT "IT REFUSED A GOOD MONTH" — that is loud and someone complains — IT IS "IT ACCEPTED A MONTH A CENT OUT", which nobody ever sees.** So the sweep asserts that over ~320 books/outstanding/unmatched combinations, a genuinely tying month completes and **a month one cent out in EITHER direction is refused**. ✅ **It holds.** Five mutations, five reds: the tolerance widened to a cent · an unverified balance no longer blocking · zero passing without confirmation · **zero-rows treated as success (the C194 bug itself)** · the completion reading React state instead of the synchronous ref (the seam that once inserted a SECOND row).
  - **★ AND THE ONE FAILURE WAS MY FIXTURE AGAIN, FOR THE SAME REASON AS THE DEPRECIATION SWEEP: I used a books balance of `33333.335`.** A GL balance is a **sum of two-decimal amounts**, so a third decimal cannot occur — the fixture invented an impossible input and then blamed the gate. Recorded because it is now the second time the same wrong instinct produced a false alarm: **money has two decimals, and a sweep that forgets it tests arithmetic nothing will ever be handed.**

- **★★★ C292 — THE RE-UPLOAD DEDUP HOLDS, AND MY FIRST TWO FIXTURES COULD NOT HAVE PROVED IT (2026-08-31).** `O83` is the worst production incident this project has had: re-uploading one statement double-booked **14 of 20 lines**, because the dedup key mixed the CLEANED vendor on the booked side with the RAW memo on the re-parse side — two strings that never collide. The key is now date + amount + direction only. **Swept: book a statement, re-parse the SAME statement, every line must come back marked.** ✅ **It holds on every shape** — ordinary, three identical same-day lines, opposing directions, awkward cents, a month boundary — and correctly does NOT mark a genuinely new line, a booking on another account, or one whose entry was voided.
  - **★★★ AND THE MUTATION THAT MATTERED: PUTTING THE DESCRIPTION BACK IN THE KEY — THE O83 BUG ITSELF — LEFT EVERY TEST GREEN.** My fixture passed the **same** description to both sides, so there was no asymmetry to expose. **A fixture that agrees with itself proves nothing** (the `·3a` shape, third time this project has recorded it). Fixed by reproducing what production actually does — the booked row carries a cleaned vendor, a rewritten memo and a re-categorised GL — and then **restoring the bug faithfully in the CALLER**, which fails 9 tests with the incident's own words: *"3 of 3 lines would be booked AGAIN"*.
  - **★★ IT ALSO CAUGHT A VACUOUS ASSERTION I HAD WRITTEN TWO COMMITS EARLIER.** `gaapSweep`'s bank-line case passed `buildBankLineEntry`'s output straight to a balance check that SKIPS anything without a `lines` array — and that function returns the flattened ROW, not a `{lines}` entry. **So the bank-line half of that sweep asserted nothing.** Now expanded exactly as the write path posts it, with a count assertion so it cannot silently skip again.
  - **★ MY FIRST MUTATION WAS ALSO WRONG, AND SAYING SO IS THE DISCIPLINE:** I added a description to `bankTxnKey`, which receives only three fields — so it was empty on both sides and changed nothing. **A mutation that cannot express the bug is not evidence the bug is guarded against.**

- **★★★ C291 — THE LARGE-CHARGE DETECTOR WAS FLAGGING THE RENT, EVERY MONTH (2026-08-30).** The detectors had only ever been tested on fixtures built to TRIP them. That proves each one CAN fire and says **nothing** about whether they fire on ordinary bookkeeping — which is the failure that costs, because a client asked the same pointless question every month stops reading the questions.
  - **★★★ THE FIND: monthly rent raised `large_transaction` EVERY MONTH** — *"If it's equipment or software lasting over a year, it may need to be capitalized"*, asked of the most predictable expense a business has. **`O122`'s rule verbatim: a card the user sees every month is a bug wearing a question mark.**
  - **★★ FIXED BY A PROPERTY, NOT A THIRD SPECIAL CASE.** `isSystemPostedEntry` was added to this same detector because PAYROLL tripped it; rent is not payroll, so it was not covered. The real property in both cases is that **a vendor with an established history at this magnitude is not a surprise** — and `vendor_spike` already owns *"this vendor charged unusually much"*, so this detector is for a large charge we have no history for. **Two priors within 25% is the bar: one is a coincidence, two is a pattern.**
  - **★★ A SURVIVING MUTATION SHOWED MY TESTS COULD NOT TELL `< 2` FROM `< 1`** — the spike fixture's amounts were nowhere near its priors, so the count was 0 either way. **An unpinned judgement drifts**, so the bar is now asserted directly (0 priors → fires · 1 → fires · 2 → silent). Pinning it needed a second correction: counting the TYPE total failed on its own noise, because the earlier priors are themselves large and raise their own cards — it counts the SUBJECT's card now.
  - **✅ A HEALTHY 8-MONTH LEDGER NOW RAISES ZERO CARDS**, including a flat weekly vendor (`O117`), twice-monthly payroll at a round number, and identical monthly rent — and the detectors still fire on a real duplicate, a real spike and a genuinely stopped recurring charge.
  - **★ TWO SCOPE LIMITS RECORDED RATHER THAN CLAIMED AS DEFECTS:** `missing_recurring` keys on 25–40 day gaps and says so in its own copy, so a WEEKLY vendor that stops is not caught; and it needs TWO charges inside a 95-day window, so a vendor that stopped four months ago raises nothing. Both are defensible; my fixture missed the window twice before I read the code properly.

- **★★★ C290 — THE DATE SWEEP, AND THE MOST USEFUL FINDING WAS ABOUT THE SWEEP ITSELF (2026-08-30).** Dates are the axis this codebase has been bitten on most — the helpers carry the scars in their comments (never `toISOString()` for a calendar date; clamp Jan 31 + 1 month to Feb 28 rather than overflowing to Mar 3). Those lessons are applied by careful reasoning at a handful of call sites; this checks them **at every value**: ~340 dates across leap and non-leap years × 0–36 months, day round-trips, every month's first and last day against a signed period, 12 fiscal year-ends.
  - **✅ THE HELPERS HOLD. No defect found** — which is a result, not a blank: the reasoning in those comments is correct at every boundary, including month-end stickiness (`Jan 31 + 2` is `Mar 31`, not `Feb 28` carried forward — a cumulative clamp would drift every lease and asset onto the 28th forever).
  - **★★★ AND THEN THE MUTATION THAT SURVIVED: switching `ymdLocal` to `toISOString().slice(0,10)` — THE EXACT MISTAKE ITS COMMENT WARNS AGAINST — left every assertion GREEN.** It fails only from a zone AHEAD of UTC, because a Date built from local components at midnight reads back as the PREVIOUS day in UTC. **CI runs UTC.** So the guard for the bug this file exists to catch was inert exactly where it matters — the "guard whose input is always empty" shape, committed inside a test written to prevent it.
  - **★★ FIXED BY FORCING THE TIMEZONE RATHER THAN HOPING FOR ONE.** `process.env.TZ` does take effect at runtime (verified via `getTimezoneOffset`, and the block asserts the change took, or it proves nothing). The mutation now dies under `TZ=UTC`.
  - **★ AND IT FOUND ONE TIMEZONE-FRAGILE TEST IN THE EXISTING SUITE.** `anomalies.test.js` pinned `new Date("2026-01-31T12:00:00Z")` — noon UTC, which is already **1am on 1 February in Auckland** — while asserting a LOCAL-month outcome. The product is right (`thisMonth` is deliberately local, to match how entry dates are stored); the fixture was pinning a UTC instant against a local expectation. **The full suite now passes from five timezones spanning UTC−9 to UTC+13.**

- **★★ C289 — THE TIE-OUT THAT GATES SIGN-OFF CANNOT FAIL ON THE COMMONEST ENTRY SHAPE (2026-08-30).** Swept `computeControlTotals` in **both directions** — no check may fire on correct books, and each must actually fire on a broken ledger, because a control that never fires is indistinguishable from clean books (`C195(7)`, and `C241` where the sales-tax check ran BACKWARDS for its whole life under a green single-fixture suite).
  - **✅ NO FALSE FIRING:** across four magnitudes, three tax rates and three ledger shapes, every check ties on correct books; an empty company ties rather than reporting a mismatch.
  - **★★★ THE FINDING: A ONE-SIDED **TWO-LINE** ENTRY IS INVISIBLE TO `trial_balance`.** `flattenJournalEntries` collapses a 2-line entry into ONE row carrying the primary amount and **derives the offset from that same number**, so the discrepancy is discarded before the check can see it. **A 3-line entry IS caught** (verified both ways). So the check billed as *"the fundamental tie-out"* cannot fail on the commonest shape in the ledger.
  - **★ NOT CURRENTLY REACHABLE, AND THAT IS NOT THE SAME AS SAFE:** `post_journal_entry` and `buildJournalEntry` both enforce balance at write time — **but the whole point of a control total is to catch what the write path missed**, and the QuickBooks import inserts rows directly rather than through the RPC.
  - **PINNED RATHER THAN FIXED, with the reason: the repair is in `flattenJournalEntries` or in what the control is fed, both core reporting paths.** That is a deliberate change, not a rider on a sweep — and the test says so, including that it should be deleted if the limit is ever closed.

- **★★★ C288 — SWEEPING THE REPORTING LAYER FOUND A VOID THAT NEVER REACHED THE DATABASE (2026-08-30).** The same method again — take invariants checked on one fixture and vary the ledger instead. Forty generated ledgers, deterministic, each balanced BY CONSTRUCTION, through `flattenJournalEntries` into the derivations a client actually reads.
  - **★★★ THE FIND: `ReconView`'s `voidBook` CHANGED NOTHING BUT THE SCREEN.** It set `status:"voided"` via `setInvoices` in **local state only**, **wrote an audit row saying the entry was voided**, and never touched the database. So the entry returned on the next reload, the reconciliation was computed against books that did not match storage, and **the audit trail recorded a removal that never happened** — worse than a no-op. It now goes through `removeEntry`, the single funnel (`O130`) that decides soft-delete vs dated reversal by whether the month is signed, and says so when the write does not land.
  - **★★★ AND THE SECOND HALF: `flattenJournalEntries` HARDCODED `status: "booked"`.** It carried `deleted_at` (fixed in `C207`) and **never read `e.status`** — so a database-level `status = 'voided'` was invisible to net income, GL balances and the trial balance alike. **The other half of the same omission, and the shape that guard exists for: a filter is only as good as the field reaching it.**
  - **★★ THREE OF THE FOUR FIRST FAILURES WERE MY FIXTURE, AND NAMING WHICH IS THE WHOLE DISCIPLINE.** I called `trialBalance` with accounts where it takes options · I dated entries past the as-of while asserting `prior + current === all-time` · and — the good one — **my entry ids contained an underscore, which is the sentinel meaning "an expanded multi-line row"**, so `glAccountBalance` counted primary-only and every offset leg vanished. A fixture colliding with a structural convention looks exactly like a bug in the code.
  - **★★ ONE REAL DIVERGENCE IS PINNED RATHER THAN FIXED, WITH THE REASON: `computeNetIncome` and `fiscalYearSplit` disagree on an entry carrying P&L accounts on BOTH legs** (0 vs −100). **No builder in `src/lib` produces that shape** — every one pairs a P&L leg with a balance-sheet leg — so it is unreachable today. Changing a core reporting function for a case nothing can create is the wrong risk; **the test states the divergence, so if a builder ever emits that shape it fails first.**

- **★★★ C287 — THE GAAP GUARDRAIL TESTS ONE FIXTURE PER EVENT, WHICH CANNOT FIND A ROUNDING DEFECT (2026-08-30).** `gaapInvariants.test.js` asserts Dr = Cr and the accounting equation over a list of EVENTS — **one instance each, 28 distinct numbers in the whole file.** That is the right shape for *"does this builder use the correct accounts"* and it is **structurally unable to catch a defect that appears at particular VALUES**.
  - **★★★ AND C286 IS THE PROOF, DEMONSTRATED RATHER THAN ARGUED: with the ASC 842 bug restored, `gaapInvariants` PASSES and the sweep FAILS.** That entry had been unbalanced by a cent at certain payment/term/rate combinations for as long as it existed, and `buildJournalEntry` refuses an unbalanced entry — so the lease never posted, silently, under a green guardrail.
  - **`tests/gaapSweep.test.js` covers the axis the guardrail cannot vary:** every builder over 17 awkward amounts (exact thirds, half-cent boundaries, long decimals, very large and very small) and 8 rates. **Not a replacement — the other axis.**
  - **★ IT FOUND A SECOND, SMALLER ONE: `buildDepreciationSchedule` COULD RETURN A SCHEDULE WITH HOLES IN IT.** `buildDepreciationEntry` returns null when the amount rounds to nothing — a sub-dollar asset over a long life — and the null was pushed into the array. **Both consumers walk it directly** (`persistMultiLineEntry(je)` and a `.map(je => je.…)`), so it would have thrown rather than degraded. A zero-amount month is not a schedule row; the correct schedule for such an asset is one real entry in the final month, which is what it now produces.
  - **★★ TWO OF THE THREE FIRST FAILURES WERE MY TEST, NOT THE CODE, AND SAYING WHICH IS THE POINT.** I called two builders with parameter names they do not have, and I asserted a depreciation schedule sums to the RAW cost when some sweep values carry three decimals on purpose — **a third of a cent is not money**, and the schedule correctly works in cents. Comparing to the unrounded input would have been testing my fixture rather than the code.

- **★★★ C286 — `O89` THIRD SLICE FOUND A LEASE THAT SILENTLY NEVER POSTED (2026-08-30).** `handleContractFile` was 188 lines with four awaits and **no planner calls**: after the model extracts the terms, ~130 lines of ASC 842 / ASC 606 logic built the Day 1 entry and every monthly entry inline. `calcASC842` (the present-value maths) was already a tested library function; **turning its schedule into JOURNAL ENTRIES was not — and that is the half that decides what reaches the books.** Now `src/lib/contractEntries.js`; the caller is 62 lines.
  - **★★★ THE BUG, FOUND BY SWEEPING RATHER THAN BY READING: THE LEASE COMMENCEMENT ENTRY DID NOT BALANCE.** `calcASC842` rounded `rouAsset`, `currentPortion` and `nonCurrentPortion` **independently**. Current + non-current equals the liability exactly in float, but two separate roundings can land a cent away from the rounded liability — **$1,500 × 24 months @ 5% gives Dr 34,190.85 against Cr 34,190.84.**
  - **★★★ AND THE FAILURE MODE IS SILENCE, WHICH IS WHY IT SURVIVED: `buildJournalEntry` REFUSES an unbalanced entry**, so that lease's commencement simply never posted. **No wrong number on a report — a missing one.** Fixed at the source: the non-current portion is now the **plug** (`rounded liability − rounded current`), which is both the standard accounting treatment and the only version that ties by construction.
  - **★★ I PREDICTED THE WRONG BUG, AND SAYING SO IS THE POINT.** I expected the FINANCE-LEASE month to break — it debits interest AND principal, independently rounded, against one payment credit. It does not: `calcASC842` computes `principal = payment − interest` exactly, so those two rounding errors mirror. **That one is safe by coincidence rather than by construction** (an exact half-cent tie would still break it), so the sweep now guards it too. **The suspicion was wrong and the method that tested it found a real defect one line away.**
  - **★ THE SWEEP REFUSES TO PASS VACUOUSLY** — `expect(checked).toBeGreaterThan(2000)`, verified by emptying the loop with the guard left intact. *(My first attempt at that mutation changed the guard AND the loop, so it "survived" meaninglessly — a mutation that removes its own detector proves nothing.)*

- **★★★ C285 — `O89` SECOND SLICE, AND IT FOUND A DOUBLE-CLEARING PATH (2026-08-30).** Measuring the big functions by *decision logic still inside them* rather than by line count: `handleBankFile` and `bookBankTransactions` already call planners and are mostly I/O, but **`runMatchingEngine` was 126 lines with 2 awaits, 2 state writes and ZERO planner calls** — nearly pure logic living in a component. The accept/refuse rules are now `src/lib/llmMatchFilter.js`.
  - **★★ EVERY RULE IN IT EXISTS BECAUSE SOMETHING WENT WRONG ONCE, AND NONE COULD BE EXERCISED WHERE THEY LIVED.** Never re-match a line the deterministic pass took · never re-claim an open item · and **refuse a proposal whose counterpart cannot be resolved in the open universe** — O83 February rendered an empty *"MATCHING AGAINST"* panel on a 99%-confidence proposal, asking a person to confirm a match against an entity they could not see. **A proposal nobody can look at is not a proposal.**
  - **★★★ ONE DELIBERATE BEHAVIOUR CHANGE, STATED AS ONE RATHER THAN SMUGGLED IN AS A REFACTOR: THE OLD LOOP NEVER ADDED AN ACCEPTED BANK LINE TO `handledBankIds`.** It was seeded from the deterministic pass and never updated, so **two model proposals naming the SAME bank line with different open items were BOTH accepted** — and `autoCleared` feeds `planBankImport`, so that clears the same money twice. Splitting one line across proposals is not how partials are modelled here (`amount_remaining` lives INSIDE a match), so a second proposal for a taken line is a model error. **Now refused and counted**, with a test asserting the guard does not block two proposals on genuinely different lines.
  - **★ DROPS ARE RETURNED, NOT JUST LOGGED.** They were `console.warn` only, so *"the model proposed nothing usable"* and *"the model proposed nothing"* were indistinguishable — the same shape as a guard whose input is always empty.

- **★★ C284 — `O89`: THE HIGHEST-TRAFFIC DECISION IN THE PRODUCT COULD NOT BE TESTED (2026-08-30).** The census argued against a general split — median declaration 14 lines, 225 of 237 unremarkable, *"a normal component with a dozen very large functions in it"* — so the useful move is lifting the DECISION out of the biggest one and leaving the I/O. The invoice-building step inside `processUploadItem` (920 lines) is what **every invoice a client drops** runs through, and it was a closure reading `rc`, `rn`, `Date.now()` and the surrounding scope: **its rules could only be verified by reading them.** Now `src/lib/uploadedInvoice.js`, pure, `id` and `booked_at` injected.
  - **★★★ THREE LOAD-BEARING RULES, NOW PINNED AND ALL THREE MUTATION-KILLED:** a **vendor rule beats the model** and carries 99 so the confidence gate passes it · **`type` is classified from the GL CODE, never from the model's own `type` field** — the same basis `flattenJournalEntries` uses, so an odd AI answer cannot mis-slot the row out of the transactions tab the moment it books · **the fallback is Miscellaneous**, which is exactly the account `autoBookDecision` refuses to auto-book into when the vendor has a name.
  - **★★ AND THE MISCELLANEOUS PAIR IS THE INTERESTING ONE: THE FALLBACK IS CORRECT HERE.** Stopping it is the booking GATE's job, and a test now asserts both halves together — the builder produces `7100`, and `autoBookDecision` refuses it **even at confidence 100**. Separately each looks wrong; together they turn *"we couldn't tell"* into a question rather than a silent wrong bucket.
  - **★ I SUSPECTED A DEFECT HERE AND THERE WASN'T ONE — WORTH RECORDING RATHER THAN QUIETLY DROPPING.** A first search suggested the invoice path never called `autoBookDecision`; the call was simply outside my search window. **The guard is correctly placed** — after the confidence floor, before the materiality flag, and NOT gated on the score, so a learned-vendor boost to 100 cannot defeat the account check. A suspicion checked and withdrawn is a result.
  - **★ THE EXTRACTION WAS VERIFIED BY WHAT IT LEFT BEHIND:** after removing the `const confidence` declaration, a scan for bare identifier uses in the remaining loop found two — **both inside comments.** A free identifier is a runtime crash the build cannot see, and `missingImports` only covers lib exports.

- **★★★ C282 — `O102`: SHADOW MODE IS RUNNABLE AND SCORED, AND IT BOOKS NOTHING (2026-08-30).** The ladder has been built since `C201` and **invoked by nothing** — which is the same state as not existing (`C195(7)`: a mechanism nobody triggers is indistinguishable from one with nothing to do). `runShadowCalibration` runs it over a date range; `shadowReport.js` scores the result against Amendment A §5 into **PROCEED / STOP / AMBIGUOUS**.
  - **★★★ IT RUNS THE PASS TWICE OVER IDENTICAL INPUT, ON PURPOSE.** §4.1(3) makes a verdict that varies across runs an automatic fail — *KNOWN is a persistent STATE, not a recomputation* — and **with a single pass that check could never fire**: one run looks exactly like two agreeing runs. The second is a `dryRun` so the stored rows are not doubled.
  - **★★★ AND IT WILL NOT GRANT ITSELF A HUMAN'S SIGN-OFF.** §4.2's itemised disagreement review and clearing a merge candidate are CPA judgements; **absent means UNRESOLVED, never "fine"**, and unresolved items produce STOP. A report that defaulted those to done would be the vacuous pass this whole programme exists to prevent.
  - **★★ A KEY COVERING TWO NAMES IS FLAGGED, NOT ADJUDICATED.** Usually it is the resolver working (a bank string and an invoice string for one supplier); it is a MERGE only when they are different businesses, which a person decides. **Claiming to detect a merge automatically would be inventing certainty about the one failure that is invisible.**
  - **★ THIN EVIDENCE IS NEITHER A PASS NOR A FAIL — but the order matters.** Under 20 scored lines is AMBIGUOUS, checked AFTER the hard fails, so **a phantom in a thin month still STOPS rather than hiding behind "not enough data"**. Mutation-tested in that exact direction.
  - **★★ AND A SURVIVING MUTATION FOUND A REAL GAP IN MY VARIANCE CHECK.** My test only ever varied the VERDICT, so dropping `proposed_account_id` from the comparison key changed nothing. **A line can read "agree" in both runs while proposing a different account each time** — same label, different answer, and exactly the flapping §4.1(3) forbids. Now pinned.
  - **▶ WHAT REMAINS IS THE OPERATOR'S:** run it over two signed months including August and read the report. `C203` — the switch to booking authority — happens only after it says PROCEED.

- **★★ C281 — WIRING THE SUGGESTER FOUND A FALSE SUCCESS NEXT DOOR (2026-08-30).** `O106`'s suggester had no reader, which is the very defect this file spends pages on — so it now sits on the Vendors page, and confirming writes an alias through **the same `persistContact` + `validateAlias` path the vendor form already uses**: one door for a merge, always with a person behind it. A test asserts the block contains no client and exactly one write call.
  - **★★★ AND `persistContact` RETURNED NOTHING.** My confirmation checked `r.ok === false`, which could never fire — so a link that silently failed to save would have shown *"now counts as ✓"* while the two names stayed split, **and the pair would have vanished from the suggestion list**, making it unrepeatable. Caught by reading the function rather than by a test, and it is the C194 family exactly: a success sentence gated on a click instead of on a record.
  - **★ IT REPORTS NOW (`{ ok, error, row }`), and existing callers ignoring the value are unaffected** — the change only lets a caller that wants to claim success actually check. The dismissal is written **after** the verdict, so a failed link stays on the list.
  - **★ DISMISSALS ARE PER-DEVICE (`localStorage`), STATED AS A TRADE RATHER THAN LEFT AS AN OVERSIGHT:** storing them durably means a column or a schema hack, a re-shown suggestion costs one glance, and nothing financial is held there. **A CONFIRMED merge is durable, because it becomes an alias on the contact.**

- **★★ C280 — `O106`: SUGGESTING THE SAME SUPPLIER UNDER A NEW NAME, AND THE DESIGN IS ENTIRELY IN WHAT IT REFUSES (2026-08-30).** `O111` gave a person a way to say *"these two names are one supplier"*; they still had to NOTICE. This proposes the pairs.
  - **★★★ A SUGGESTER THAT PROPOSES A KNOWN-WRONG MERGE IS WORSE THAN NO SUGGESTER**, because people stop reading it and then accept a bad one out of habit — and a merge is a **one-way door** that launders one vendor's attested mapping onto another's charges, silently. `SYSCO` / `SYSCO FUEL` and `Lone Star` / `Lone Star Restaurant Supply` are pairs `vendorIdentity.test.js` already forbids merging, so **they must not be suggested either**, and both are pinned as negative cases.
  - **★★★ THE DISCRIMINATOR IS *WHAT THE EXTRA WORDS ARE*, NOT HOW SIMILAR THE STRINGS LOOK.** A legal form or a payment-rail word never changes who a business is; **one novel noun says what it sells.** `Franklin Ave Properties` vs `…LP RENT` and `SYSCO` vs `SYSCO FUEL` are about EQUALLY similar as strings — **no similarity score can separate them**, which is why this is a vocabulary question and not a scoring one. The sets are small, explicit, pinned by exact contents, and the words a business sells are asserted ABSENT.
  - **★ AND `LEGAL_FORM_WORDS` IS MOSTLY BELT-AND-BRACES, WHICH THE COMMENT NOW SAYS RATHER THAN LETTING IT LOOK LOAD-BEARING:** `normalizeName` already strips the common forms, so *"Alamo Ice LLC"* and *"Alamo Ice"* are one vendor before this module sees them. **My first test expected a suggestion there** — which would have meant asking a person to confirm something the system had already decided correctly.
  - **★★ A SURVIVING MUTATION EXPOSED A VACUOUS TEST OF MINE.** The word-order case used two names with EQUAL token counts, so an earlier guard rejected the pair and the order check was never reached — an order-blind replacement changed nothing. Fixed to a long side with genuinely more tokens, **and paired with the positive case**, so it cannot pass by rejecting everything.
  - **★ IT SUGGESTS AND NEVER DECIDES:** a test asserts the module contains no client, no write, and no path to an account; and a pair already confirmed OR declined is never asked again — re-proposing something declined is how a queue teaches people to ignore it.

- **★★★ C279 — `O113a` WROTE A FIELD WITH NO READER, AND IT WAS THE ANSWER TO `O113b` (2026-08-30).** The proxy computes the remaining budget to make its own decision, returns it on every SUCCESSFUL call (`x-ratelimit-remaining-ai` / `-upload`), and **deliberately exposes both through CORS so the browser can read them.** Nothing in `src/` ever did. **The §9 "name the reader" defect, committed by the commit that fixed the limiter** — a value written faithfully for days with nobody consulting it.
  - **★★ AND THE READER IS THE POINT OF `O113b`, NOT A NICETY.** The recorded finding is that the limit is not *"20 documents"* but **"20 documents minus whatever else you did this hour"**, which no user can compute — so the same action succeeds or fails on invisible prior spend from a different feature. **The fix for THAT is not a bigger number; it is a number you can see.** Until now the ceiling was discoverable only by hitting it.
  - **★★★ IT REPORTS DOCUMENTS, NEVER API CALLS.** A person has a stack of invoices, not a quota of HTTP requests — and since the two buckets bind at different rates, the honest figure is **the smaller of the two**, which also means `O113b`'s *"both walls sit at 20 files"* stops being something the user has to know. **The per-document cost is the CONSERVATIVE path** (3 AI calls, the invoice path, not the statement path's 2): a budget display that overstates is worse than none, because it is trusted right up to the moment it is wrong.
  - **★★ A MISSING HEADER MEANS "WE DO NOT KNOW", NEVER ZERO.** Zero is a claim that you are out, and making it because a header was absent would be `O98` on a surface whose entire job is to be steered by. An unknown bucket is skipped rather than assumed generous, and an unknown budget says nothing at all.
  - **★ A 429 IS ALSO A READING:** it names the bucket that ran out, so that one is recorded as zero — otherwise the count sits stale at its last healthy value precisely when it matters most.
  - **★ AND BEING OUT IS NOT REPORTED AS FAILURE.** *"We've read as much as we can this hour. Your remaining 7 documents are saved and will carry on automatically."* Nothing is lost — the drain picks them up — and a sentence that omitted that would recreate `O97`'s red-wall problem in words. Four mutations, four reds.

- **★★★ C297 — O107 CAUSE 2: TYPING RE-RENDERED THE ENTIRE APPLICATION (2026-08-31).** The chat text was state on the ROOT component, so **every keystroke rebuilt the 407-key context object and re-rendered every mounted view**. That is the mechanism behind *"it feels sluggish while I use it"*, as distinct from cause 1's *"it takes a while to load"*. **It was read in exactly ONE place** (`handleChatSend`) while **every view destructured it unused** — copy-paste, paid for on every character typed.
  - **★★★ FIXED BY LOCALITY, NOT MEMOISATION, AND THE DIFFERENCE IS THE WHOLE POINT.** Memoising a 407-key context needs a complete dependency list, and a missed dependency means **silently stale numbers on an accounting screen** — the worst failure this product can have. Moving the state into the only component that reads it **has no such failure mode**: nothing else can see it, so nothing else can go stale. A cheaper fix with a strictly smaller blast radius.
  - **★★ THE TWO THINGS THAT STILL HAD TO REACH THE BOX WERE KEPT, AND NEITHER COSTS A KEYSTROKE.** A Reports button pre-fills a question — now a `chatPrefill` channel that changes on a CLICK — and it is **keyed on the prefill's own timestamp**, because a plain string would be ignored the second time and read as the button being broken. A company switch clears the box by **remounting the composer** rather than reaching into it.
  - **★★ AND THE BOX ONLY CLEARS WHEN THE SEND IS ACCEPTED.** `handleChatSend` returns false when it declines (already loading, empty after trim); clearing regardless would discard what someone had typed.
  - **★ THE MISSING IMPORT WAS CAUGHT BY THE GUARD, NOT THE BUILD.** My edit added `<ChatComposer />` without its import — Vite does not resolve free identifiers, so the build passed. `noUndefinedRefs` failed immediately. Second time this session that guard has earned its place.

- **★★★ C277 — O107 CAUSE 1 FIXED: LOADING A COMPANY WAS ELEVEN ROUND TRIPS IN SERIES, AND THE SERIAL VERSION HAD A ROBUSTNESS BUG NOBODY HAD NOTICED (2026-08-30).** `loadAllData` awaited eleven independent table reads one after another — none needs another's result — so the wall-clock cost was the **SUM of eleven latencies where it can be the MAXIMUM**: roughly 1–3 seconds of pure waiting on every load and every company switch, none of it computation. **18 awaits → 3.**
  - **★★★ AND THE SPEED IS THE LESSER HALF: A SINGLE FAILING TABLE STARVED EVERY TABLE AFTER IT.** A `documents` error meant `reconciliations`, contracts, the audit log and the AI profile **never loaded at all** — silently, underneath a comment asserting that a secondary fetch failing *"degrades gracefully"*. It did not; it degraded everything downstream of it. **`Promise.all` would have preserved that flaw exactly** (first rejection discards every sibling result), which is why it is `allSettled` and why a test forbids `Promise.all(` in this function.
  - **★ A REJECTED READ YIELDS `{}`, so every consumer takes the same "no data" branch it always took** — the change is where the waiting happens, not what any mapping does.
  - **★★ AND I INTRODUCED A BUG WHILE FIXING IT, CAUGHT BY WRITING THE TEST RATHER THAN THE CODE.** The new mid-flight company-switch guard returns from INSIDE the `try` — which still runs the `finally`, marking data "loaded" for a company the user has switched away from. **That is precisely the false emptiness the CR-18 comment at the top of the same function refuses to allow**, reintroduced twenty lines below it. The `finally` is now conditional on still being on that company.
  - Three mutations, three reds: `all` for `allSettled` · the stale-company guard removed from the `finally` · a rejected read no longer degrading to no-data.

- **★★ C275 — A REAL BANK ACCOUNT LOOKED LIKE THE ONE WE SEED, SO ONBOARDING COULD NEVER FINISH (2026-08-30). NO MIGRATION NEEDED — THE FACT WAS ALREADY RECORDED.**
  - `isPlaceholderBank` decided *"this is the row we seeded, not a real account"* from its NAME plus the absence of details — so **a genuine account actually called "Primary Checking", with no bank name, no last four and no balance yet, was indistinguishable from the seed**, and the onboarding step *"add your bank account"* could never be ticked. Nothing on screen explained why. **Editing it was the user's own assertion that the account is theirs; the code had no way to hear it.**
  - **★★ AND THE ANSWER WAS ALREADY IN THE SCHEMA: `bank_accounts` CARRIES A `set_updated_at` TRIGGER.** The seeded row is inserted once and never touched, so its two timestamps are identical; the first save moves `updated_at` and it stays moved. **A migration was drafted in my head and then not needed** — worth recording, because the reflex here is to add a column (`accounts.origin`, `documents.document_date`) and the cheaper move is to check whether the fact is already being written.
  - **★ AN UNPARSEABLE OR MISSING TIMESTAMP PAIR MEANS "WE DO NOT KNOW", NOT "IT WAS EDITED".** The other direction would declare every account real on any company whose rows predate the timestamps — **ticking off a step the person never did**, which is the same false-green family as everything else in this file. The old heuristic stays in charge when we cannot tell.
  - **★ AND A SECOND'S TOLERANCE, NOT EQUALITY:** both timestamps come from the same `now()` on insert, and a test pins that insert-time jitter is not an edit while five seconds is.

- **★★★ C274 — MY OWN UNCHECKED-WRITE GUARD PASSED FOR THE WRONG REASON (2026-08-30).** `C249` made the rule mechanical: every row-targeted `.update()` must carry `.select()` or go through the helper, and **`bank_accounts` is on the list of tables that may never be excused.** It reported clean anyway.
  - **★★ THE HOLE: THE SCAN TOOK A FIXED 5-LINE WINDOW, AND `persistBankAccounts`' UNCHECKED `.update()` SITS TWO LINES ABOVE AN UNRELATED `.insert(...).select(...)`.** The window borrowed the *insert's* `.select()` and cleared the *update*. **A guard that passes for the wrong reason is worse than no guard, because it gets cited as evidence** — and it was: C249's report said the class was closed. The chain now ends at its own semicolon.
  - **★ THE FIXED SCAN IMMEDIATELY FOUND A SECOND WRITE — ON `journal_entry_lines`, the table the guard's own comment calls unexcusable — AND IT WAS A FALSE POSITIVE WORTH KEEPING.** `persistChatRetagProject` re-reads the rows and rejects unless EVERY one carries the new value: that verifies the VALUE, where rows-affected only verifies that something was touched. **A read-back is a STRONGER check than `.select()`, not a weaker one**, so the guard learned to recognise it rather than force correct money-path code into a lint shape — and it requires the read-back AND a branch on it, because **a read-back nobody acts on is just a wasted query.**
  - **★★ AND THE WRITE ITSELF WAS THE C247 CLASS: IT SAVES THE USER'S OWN TYPING.** The account name, the bank, the last four digits, the balance — silently lost, inside a `console.warn` catch, with no reason for them to suspect it and no way to notice until the value was simply gone. It is now checked, audited, and **names which account failed**, because *"something went wrong"* does not tell someone which of their edits to redo.

- **★★ C273 — RECONCILE STOPPED ASKING FOR A STATEMENT IT ALREADY HELD (2026-08-30), AND THE GUARD THAT SHOULD HAVE CAUGHT MY BUG DID NOT.**
  - **The handover path (`reconcileOffer`) knew the lines were parsed and stored; the TAB did not.** So a CPA who navigated to Reconcile rather than following the link was asked to re-upload a file the system read days ago — **and ReconView carries its own second CSV parser to read it with**, which is the two-implementations-of-one-contract shape this codebase keeps paying for. `statementToOffer` names the stored statement and its line count; the drop zone stays for a statement we genuinely do not hold.
  - **★ IT REFUSES TO OFFER A MONTH A COMPLETED RECONCILIATION ALREADY COVERS**, using `reconciliationCoversStatement` — the same predicate the auto-complete path uses, so the two cannot drift into disagreeing about what *"already done"* means. And **the count is fetched, never assumed**: `statementOfferCopy` says less rather than inventing a number, because the number is the whole reason to trust the offer.
  - **★★★ AND THE INTERESTING PART IS A MISS: I USED `monthLabel` IN ReconView WITHOUT IMPORTING IT, THE BUILD PASSED, AND `viewsRender.test.jsx` PASSED TOO.** Vite does not resolve free identifiers, so it is a runtime crash the build cannot see — **and the render sweep missed it because the guilty call sits in a branch that only renders when a statement exists, which the render fixture has none of.** That is C246's own recorded limitation (*"catches a screen that crashes, not one that silently renders empty"*) meeting reality inside a day.
  - **`tests/missingImports.test.js` closes it, scoped narrowly on purpose:** does a component call something `src/lib` exports without importing it? A general undefined-identifier check is a linter and would drown in false positives; this asks one question with almost no room for one. **Verified by removing the exact import I forgot and watching it fail.**
  - **★★ AND IT TOOK THREE TRIES BECAUSE PROSE LIVES IN MORE PLACES THAN COMMENTS.** First version flagged `rows.push(...)` — a method, not a free identifier. Second flagged `badge (only when signed off)` inside a **JSX block comment** and `"your credit card (liability 2200)"` inside a **string literal**. **That is the sixth and seventh time a source-scanning guard in this repo has tripped on prose**, and the durable rule is now explicit: **strip block comments, line comments AND string literals before scanning** — line-prefix filtering was never going to be enough.

One input at the top of Home — *"Ask about your books, or tell us what happened…"* — opens the same chat with what was typed, through the prefill channel Reports already uses (`C297`, keyed on its own timestamp so a repeat is not ignored). **All six U-rows shipped in one sitting (C375–C380); the two Settings merges U3 named are the one piece deliberately left, as their own change.**

It compares the last two complete months now, and a prior month under $250 is not a baseline. Six tests pinned the old wording (*"You're profitable"*, *"runway"*) and were re-aimed at the framing they protect — coverage for a profitable business, a countdown for a loss-making one, never both in one sentence.

- **★★★ C374 — THE QUESTIONS COME TO THE OWNER IN THE MIDDLE OF THE SCREEN, ONE AT A TIME — AND BUILDING IT FOUND THAT `C264`'s "DANGEROUS FIRST" HAD NEVER FIRED ON A REAL CARD (2026-09-14). So every real card read as WAITS, the *dangerous first* order never once fired, and the banner never named a kind — **while `cardUrgency.test.js` passed on `{ kind: "lifecycle" }` fixtures nobody produces.** The `·3a` shape inside the guard written for `O120`, found only because the stepper was rendered with the REAL shapes and the wrong card came first. The reader keys on the flags now, and a guard holds `App.jsx`'s pushes and the reader to the same three names.

Every built upload invoice carries its upload item (`_queueItemId`) so a decision taken minutes later can find the document's row; a `partialHoldsRef` records what landed and what was expected at hold time. *(Manual and chat entries have no intake row and are untouched.)*

HELD is terminal, so `ownerTrustState`'s completeness arithmetic counted the row as done; the queue tile that said *"Needs a look"* died with the tab; and after a reload the trust panel read ***"Everything you sent is accounted for — N documents, nothing missing"*** over a file that had become nothing at all. **The `C194`/`O98` false green on the surface whose whole job is to be trusted, reachable by dropping one blurry photo and pressing F5.**

So the fuel supplier's email, phone, address and `gl_code` filled the empty fields of a different business's contact, no "SYSCO FUEL" contact was ever created, and the Vendors tab (`C317`) then showed a ledger group with no contact beside a contact wearing another business's details. Same for *Lone Star* / *Lone Star Restaurant Supply*. **Demonstrated with the shipped rule reproduced verbatim before it was called a defect.** One rule now, the SAME one the Vendors tab joins on — canonical key through the alias index, exact — in `contactMatch.js`. *(A mutation that put containment back in the direction that cannot match a longer name survived, correctly: the direction that bites is the longer name containing the shorter key, and that one is red.)*

Rows whose card IS on screen are excluded by the card's own upload item, so an open question is never counted twice; the header counts cards plus held rows.

Home says *"1 document from earlier is still waiting for an answer from you — it isn't in your books until you answer"* with **Bring the questions back**; a row with no stored file (pre-`C300`) gets the sentence and no button (`O124`).
  - **★★★ AND THE FIRST PLACEMENT WAS INSIDE `{uploadQueue.length > 0 && …}` — A CARD FOR THE MOMENT AFTER A RELOAD, GATED ON THE ONE THING A RELOAD EMPTIES.** The rendered probe, whose fixture has no upload queue, showed nothing, and that is the whole finding: `C321`'s locked room, found by the test before the commit rather than by a person after it. The probe now pins that the queue is empty when it asserts the card, and a mutation putting the gate back goes red.

- **★★ C364 — THE CATCH-ALL'S REVIEW RECORD IS DURABLE NOW; THE TABLE HAD BEEN WAITING SINCE THE BASELINE (2026-09-14). SEVEN MUTATIONS, SEVEN REDS — ONE AFTER ITS GUARD WAS PINNED TO THE BRANCH.** `C363` recorded that `unknownDocs` never touched `unknown_documents`. Reading the baseline DDL: **the table has EXACTLY the in-session record's shape** — `document_type_detected`, `ai_explanation`, `entry_needed`, `entry_summary`, `proposed_journal_entry`, `watch_for`, `watch_matches`, `posted`, `dismissed` — and `document_id NOT NULL`. Somebody designed the durable half and nobody wired either end. **`O95`'s shape at table scale: a schema built for a reader and a writer, and neither ever came.**
  - **`document_id NOT NULL` IS THE RIGHT CONSTRAINT AND IT DECIDES THE DESIGN.** A row is written only when the durable-first store (`C300`) handed back a real document id; a file we could not keep leaves an in-session record and an audit row saying so, rather than a row pointing at nothing. `unknownDocRow` returns null without one, pinned.
  - **THREE WRITES, ONE READ.** Creation goes through `insertVerified` and the record takes the row's uuid as its id; a watch match (`checkWatchTriggers`) is written with `checkedRowUpdate`, a lost write audited; the Review Post buttons write `posted` / the match's `posted` through `persistUnknownDocPatch` and **say a lost marker before the ✓** — the entry IS in the ledger, so *"don't post it again"* (`C207`). The load reads `dismissed = false` with the document's name joined; **a failed read leaves the in-session list alone** (O98: could-not-ask is not there-are-none), and a mutation that emptied it on failure went red.
  - **★ THE ROW'S KEYS ARE CHECKED AGAINST THE BASELINE DDL**, so a column the table lacks fails the suite rather than the insert — `C308`'s lesson, on the write side.
  - **★ AND THE RECORDED ESCAPE APPEARED ON SCHEDULE:** `if (false)` around the watch-match write survived a guard that matched the call. Pinned to the branch (`if (isDbUnknownDocId(doc.db_id) && …) { checkedRowUpdate(`), per `C238`/`C240`/`C248`.

- **★★ C407 — THE SOLO SIGN-OFF CARD LET YOU TICK, PRESS, AND ONLY THEN LEARN THE MONTH COULDN'T BE SIGNED — IN THE ACCOUNTANT'S WORDS (2026-09-15). TWO MUTATIONS, TWO REDS.** From the operator's screenshot: *Sign off August 2026 yourself* sat directly under *"We've asked you about 4 transactions"*, fully enabled. `signOffReadiness` refuses a month with open questions, an unmatched bank, or a control total off — and the card ran it only AFTER the click, then printed the refusal it got: *"the bank isn't reconciled yet (40 days)"*, *"3 checks that should match don't: ap_tie"*. `O124`'s rule (an impossible action is DISABLED, with the reason) and the owner bar, both broken on the one control that locks a month. `ownerSignOffBlockers` maps every net the readiness check can emit to one plain sentence (*"August 2026 hasn't been matched to your bank statement yet — drop that month's statement."*, *"2 transactions still need a look."*) and reports a net it does not know rather than dropping it — a blocker that vanishes from the list is a sign-off nobody meant; the card shows *"Not ready to sign yet:"* with the list and disables the box and the button while any remain; the click path reads the same map. *(And I truncated `TrustPanel.jsx` to three lines with a regex slip mid-edit — caught by `wc -l` before anything ran; restored from HEAD and re-applied. Tenth mechanical slip of the family: verify the edit did what it was written to do.)*

- **★ C406 — THE NUDGE BAR REPEATED THE LINE ABOVE IT WORD FOR WORD (2026-09-15).** In the operator's screenshot the *Nothing wrong* line read *"We've asked you about 4 transactions — they're not in your books until you answer"* and the gold bar under it read the same sentence again, with the button — and Home's waiting-on-you list carries a third telling. The bar says what the button does now (*"Go through them one at a time — nothing is lost if you stop."*), so the sentence appears once on the panel; rendered and counted.

- **★★ C405 — "ANSWER 4 QUESTIONS →" ON THE TRUST PANEL OPENED THE REVIEW TAB (2026-09-15). The confidence-flag nudge is genuinely a reviewer's queue and keeps its Review door (button for a reviewer, status for a client). Rendered in both seats; the `C197` pin was re-aimed at the property it protects — the REVIEW door stays cockpit-only — rather than at the nudge that used to carry it.

- **★ C404 — HOME'S "ASK ABOUT YOUR BOOKS" ASKS (2026-09-15).** `C380` opened the chat drawer with the typed question sitting in the box, waiting for a SECOND Enter — a person who had just pressed Enter on Home saw a drawer open and nothing answer, which reads as *nothing happened* (`O123`). A prefill marked `send` is sent on arrival; if the chat declines (still answering the last one) the text stays in the box exactly as before, so nothing typed is lost. Reports' *"analyse this"* button deliberately stays prefill-only — its prompt is a draft the person may edit. **And wiring it found that `C297`'s guarantee — *"the box clears only when the send is accepted"* — had never held: `handleChatSend` is `async`, so `onSend(msg) === false` compared a Promise and was never true, and a declined send cleared the box.** The composer now clears at once and REFILLS the box when the promise resolves to `false` and nothing new was typed; the property is run, not only pinned.

- **★ C403 — AND THE OTHER ELEVEN (2026-09-15). ONE MUTATION, ONE RED.** The `C401`/`C402` sweep, finished: every handler in `ERP` that moves money or attests a month now runs under one keyed in-flight guard — contract posts (`postContractEntry` posted the entry and only THEN marked it posted, so two presses inside the round trip posted twice), bank-match confirm and dismiss (`dismissMatch` read `matchQueue` from the render's closure, so the row it had just filtered out was still there for the second press — and it BOOKS), opening balances from the grid and from a statement, sign-off, the recurring suggestion's *"yes"*, and the three signed-month decisions, which share one key so *reopen* and *move to this month* cannot both act on one held entry. `…Once` bodies, one caller each, pinned by name. **Twelve source anchors in six test files moved with the signatures** — each re-aimed at the guarded body, none dropped.

- **★ C402 — THE SAME DOUBLE-PRESS ON THREE MORE MONEY BUTTONS (2026-09-15). ONE MUTATION, ONE RED.** `C401`'s shape swept across the product: *Mark paid* (`markBillPaid` — its idempotency probe reads the ledger BEFORE posting, so two presses inside one round trip both read "not yet paid" and both post), *Post* on a payroll register (`postPayroll`, no guard at all — two registers' worth of salaries from one file), and *Post now* on the Recurring screen (`runRecurring` — the due check reads `last_run`, which is written after the post). Each is now a keyed in-flight run (`makeKeyedInFlight`: one per bill, one per register, one per rule — so paying bill A never blocks paying bill B), and a test asserts nothing calls the `…Once` bodies directly.

- **★★ C401 — A DOUBLE-CLICK ON A QUESTION CARD BOOKED TWICE (2026-09-15). TWO MUTATIONS, TWO REDS.** `onPill` guarded on `done`, and `done` is set only AFTER `bookToDb` returns — so between the click and the ledger's answer (an RPC round trip, longer on a slow connection) a second click on *"different purchase"* or *"new charge"* called the writer again: two entries for one answer, on the card an owner is likeliest to press twice because it "did nothing". `O123`'s rule, in money. `makeOneInFlight` (pure, `src/lib/oneInFlight.js`) admits one run at a time — a closure, not React state, because state lags a render and the double-click lands inside that lag — and every booking path on the card runs under it; the pills, the free-text submit and the summary button disable while busy. *(The GAAP path was already safe by accident: `applyGaapAnswer` removes the card from the queue before it books, so it cannot be pressed twice — recorded so nobody "fixes" that ordering.)* Tested behaviourally: two runs inside the window write once, a settled run — success or refusal — admits the next.

- **★★ C398 — FOUR MORE REMOVALS THAT REMOVED NOTHING, FOUND BY SWEEPING `C397`'S SHAPE (2026-09-15). - **★★ C397 — THE SUPPLIER-RULES SCREEN'S "REMOVE" REMOVED NOTHING (2026-09-15). ONE MUTATION SURVIVED, THEN ONE RED.** `C360` found the Recurring screen's *"+ Add"* was a `setRecurring`; the Supplier rules screen's *Remove* was the same shape — `setRules(r => r.filter(...))`, local state only. The row vanished, came back on the next reload, and **kept coding that supplier's invoices in the meantime** — a rule the person had just deleted, still deciding their books, with nothing on screen to say so. The chat's `delete_rule` has deleted through `deleteVerified` all along; the screen now calls the same function (`removeRule`), audits on `ok`, and says a refusal. **And the mutation that survived was the `−1` trap for the fifth time**: `indexOf("if (!r?.ok) {")` compared against another index passes when the gate is gone. **Rule, since it keeps recurring: any structure pin built on `indexOf` asserts the anchor is present (`> -1`) BEFORE comparing positions.**

- **★★ C396 — THE TOASTS WERE NEVER UNDER THE OWNER BAR, AND TWELVE ON OWNER PATHS FAILED IT (2026-09-15). A census of every `showNotification` literal on an owner-reachable path found twelve: *"Booked as deferred revenue (advance payment) ✓"* and *"Capitalized & depreciation scheduled ✓"* on the question cards an owner answers; *"Journal entry not found"*, *"That entry has no debit to asset account 1500"* and *"Depreciation attached & scheduled ✓"* on the equipment-cost repair `C255` put on the owner's detail panel; *"we couldn't record this upload in the intake ledger"* and *"couldn't save the reconciliation record"* on the Home drop zone; *"Contract analyzed — 3 journal entries generated"* on an agreement an owner drops; and `C260`'s own *"added to your chart of accounts"* in the chat. All in the owner's words now (*"Recorded — its cost will be spread over time ✓"*, *"That transaction isn't a purchase of equipment (Fixed Assets), so there is no cost to spread"*, *"Agreement read — 3 entries prepared ✓"*). `tests/ownerToasts.test.js` reads every toast literal in the owner-reachable files; the four reviewer-only toasts in `App.jsx` (the contract post buttons, the manual-entry form) are excused by exact string with the screen that owns them, and a stale excuse fails. *(And the one native `confirm()` an owner can reach — the QuickBooks undo — said "soft-deletes", a word the regex does not know; it reads "will be removed from your books" now, and `confirm()` dialogs are in the census.)*

- **★ C395 — A VENDOR'S LOGO WHERE WE KNOW THEIR DOMAIN, INITIALS EVERYWHERE ELSE (2026-09-15). TWO MUTATIONS, TWO REDS.** Operator: *"can we pull in vendor logos for the vendors, or is that too much? Known vendors."* The honest scope is exactly the words *known vendors*: a logo is fetched by DOMAIN, and we know a vendor's domain in two cases — the curated universal directory (`C202`; every row now carries the brand's public domain, curated beside the mapping it already asserts) and a contact whose record carries a `website` a person typed. **No name-to-domain guessing** — *"Hill Country Milling → hillcountrymilling.com"* is the confident-looking wrong answer this file exists to refuse, and a wrong logo beside a supplier's name is a claim about who they are; a mutation that guessed goes red on the anti-merge pairs (`SYSCO FUEL`, `SQUARE DANCE HALL`). The image is the site's own icon from Google's public favicon endpoint, **stated as a trade**: the browser sends the DOMAIN to Google — a national brand's public domain, or one a person chose to record — and nothing else; `logoUrlFor` is the one function to change if that trade is ever wrong. A failed load falls back to initials, so a broken image never sits beside a name. `VendorAvatar` replaces the four hand-rolled initials blocks on the Vendors and Customers tabs; no migration — the domains live in the code seed the app already reads, and the live `universal_vendor_directory` table is untouched.

The eclipse logomark the header draws is now `public/favicon.svg` (SVG, so it is crisp at every size) with a 180px `apple-touch-icon.png` rasterised from it for Safari and home screens; Vite copies `public/` into the build, verified in `dist/`.

- **C539 — EVERY PAYMENT ON THE TRANSACTIONS LIST AND THE DETAIL PANEL WAS VENDORED "PAYMENT" (2026-09-16). ONE MUTATION, ONE RED.** `C538`'s cause, swept: a settlement's description is *"Payment – Vendor"*, the flatten's `vendor` is the left of the dash, so the Transactions list printed a **P** avatar and *Payment* in the vendor column beside *"Payment – Hill Country Milling Co."*, and the panel's headline read *Payment*. `displayParty` (`txnPresent.js`) names the party from the bill or invoice the settlement links, else from its own description's right half, and leaves every other row's vendor alone; Home's feed, the list, the panel and the chat's `search_transactions` read it (*"how much with Roma"* found the bill and missed its payment, which was vendored *Payment*). *(The account cell still shows the leg the payment cleared — `C503`'s design.)*
- **C538 — HOME'S ACTIVITY FEED CALLED A BILL PAYMENT "PAYMENT — ACCOUNTS PAYABLE", AND WOULD HAVE CALLED A CUSTOMER'S PAYMENT MONEY OUT OF "CASH" (2026-09-16). ONE MUTATION, TWO REDS.** The render fixture grew a taxed invoice and a real settlement (`C527`'s fixture, widened), and the owner-bar test on Home went red at once: the feed described every row by its own primary leg and P&L side, and a settlement's primary leg is A/P (a payment) or Cash (a collection) — so a payment printed the accounting word, a collection would have printed 🧾 *"Collection — Cash −$300"*, and both carried the vendor *"Payment"* (a settlement's description is *"Payment – Vendor"*, and the flatten's vendor is the left half). The feed reads `classifyTxn` now — the Transactions list's own classifier, so the two cannot disagree about which way money moved — and names the party from the bill or invoice the settlement links (`payment_for`): *"Paid Hill Country Milling Co. −$824.60"*, *"Received from Acme +$300.00"*. *(The C317 render pin expected the two Hill Country bills' sum in one figure; with one of them now paid it reads the row's *paid this year* and *still owed* side by side — the same property, one supplier on one row.)*
- **C537 — A RECURRING FREQUENCY THE APP CANNOT REPRESENT WAS SILENTLY MADE MONTHLY (2026-09-16). ONE MUTATION, ONE RED.** The mirror of `C535`/`C536`, and the worse direction: `buildRecurringRow` wrote `monthly` for any frequency outside the four, so a model's *biweekly* payroll rule or *yearly* insurance premium became a monthly charge — offered for posting twelve times a year — that looked correctly set up. A test pinned that clamp as a feature (*"clamps an invalid frequency to monthly"*). Synonyms are mapped (*yearly*, *every week*); anything else returns null and the chat says what it can do — *"I can't set up a charge 'biweekly' — I can do weekly, monthly, quarterly or annual"* — because the one thing worse than refusing is guessing (§12's own rule for a missing tax rate). The screen's form only offers the four and is untouched.
- **C536 — THE AI'S `add_account` WROTE ITS OWN WORD FOR THE CATEGORY, AND "Expense" IS NOT ONE OF THE FIVE (2026-09-16). TWO MUTATIONS, TWO REDS.** `accounts.category` is CHECK-constrained to `Assets | Liabilities | Equity | Revenue | Expenses`; `addCustomAccount` — the chat's `add_account` door and the Categories screen's — inserted `category || "Expenses"`, so a model writing *Expense*, *asset* or *liability* had the insert refused and the reply read *"couldn't add"*. §4's numbering convention decides the category from the code's first digit, and that is the rule now (`categoryForCode`): the code decides, a stated word is only normalised onto the five, and a word that disagrees with the code loses to it. Every account insert (`buildAccountInsert`, `addCustomAccount`) writes through it; the test parses the CHECK. *(`tests/recurringColumns.test.js` → `tests/checkConstraintWriters.test.js`: it holds `C525`, `C535` and this one.)*
- **C535 — A CONTACT THE CHAT ADDED AS A "SUPPLIER" WAS REFUSED BY THE DATABASE (2026-09-16). ONE MUTATION, ONE RED.** `contacts.type` is CHECK-constrained to `vendor | customer | both`; the model's schema says *"vendor|customer"*, and `persistChatContact` wrote whatever word arrived. A model that said *supplier* or *client* — ordinary words for the same two things — had its insert refused and the reply read *"couldn't add"*. `normalizeContactType` maps the synonyms and falls to the schema's own default; both the insert and the update branch write through it, and the test parses the CHECK so the app's list cannot drift from the column's.
- **C534 — THE PANEL'S "ATTACH SOURCE DOCUMENT" HAD NEVER STORED A FRESH FILE: IT WROTE THE ROW'S P&L SIDE AS THE DOCUMENT TYPE (2026-09-16). TWO MUTATIONS, TWO REDS.** The `C533` method — one vocabulary in several places — applied to `documents.document_type`: the column's CHECK allows seven values; the detail panel's attach button passed `inv.type || "invoice"`, and `inv.type` is `"expense"` or `"revenue"` on every flattened row, so PostgREST refused the insert and `storeDocument` fell to its in-session fallback, which `C325`'s ✓ gate correctly withheld. **That is the button `C324`/`C326` named as the one-at-a-time repair for the 28 unlinked documents** — it worked only when the file was already in the library (the dedupe branch inserts nothing) and failed on every fresh file, which is why it looked repaired sometimes. `storeDocument` maps every type through `documentTypeFor` at its one insert now (`C300`'s own rule: the taxonomy never costs the file), and the panel passes a document type — *invoice* for a sale, *receipt* for a purchase. `tests/attachDocType.test.js` parses the CHECK and holds `DOCUMENT_TYPES` and every value the mapper can produce to it. *(`C533`, same sitting: a guard that the recurring-frequency vocabulary is one set across the DB CHECK, the writer, the form and the scheduler — every allowed frequency advances.)*
- **C532 — THE SIDEBAR'S "BILLS TO PAY" BADGE COUNTED LINES AND TILL PURCHASES, AND THE CHAT'S OVERDUE LIST SPLIT A BILL IN TWO (2026-09-15). THREE MUTATIONS, THREE REDS.** The badge read `openPayables(invoices).length` — the code-free per-ROW flag list, under a comment saying it was *"the SAME source the Payables screen lists"*; the screen has read the code-aware `openPayablesGL` since `C513`. So a two-line bill counted twice, the Sabine freezer not at all, and a purchase paid at the till (`Dr Expense / Cr Cash`, no flag) as a bill to pay — a number the screen it opens could not account for, which is the sentence that comment was written to prevent. The badge reads the screen's list now. `get_overdue_invoices` had the same population (`C487` chose it code-free because the ctx *"may not carry"* a code — it does): a two-line overdue bill was two overdue bills at half each.
